Delocalization of One-Dimensional Random Band Matrices
Horng-Tzer Yau, Jun Yin

Abstract
Consider an N × N N\times N Hermitian one-dimensional random band matrix with band width W > N 1 / 2 + 𝔠 W>N^{1/2+\mathfrak{c}} for any 𝔠 > 0 {\mathfrak{c}}>0 . In the bulk of the spectrum and in the large N N limit, we obtain the following results: (i) The semicircle law holds up to the scale N − 1 + ε N^{-1+\varepsilon} for any ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . (ii) All L 2 L^{2} - normalized eigenvectors are delocalized, meaning their L ∞ L^{\infty} norms are simultaneously bounded by N − 1 2 + ε N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon} with overwhelming probability, for any ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . (iii) Quantum unique ergodicity holds in the sense that the local L 2 L^{2} mass of eigenvectors becomes equidistributed with high probability. (iv) Universality of eigenvalue statistics holds, i.e., the local eigenvalue statistics of these band matrices are given by those of Gaussian unitary ensembles.



1
Introduction

The localization-delocalization transition has been a central question in mathematical physics since Anderson’s seminal work on the tight-binding model, which essentially describes a discrete random Schrödinger operator. The localization of this operator was rigorously established over four decades ago by Fröhlich and Spencer
[
27
]
using a multi-scale analysis argument. Subsequently, a shorter proof was provided by Aizenman and Molchanov
[
1
]
via the fractional moment method. Many remarkable results concerning the localization of the Anderson model have been achieved (see, e.g.,
[
28
,
7
,
8
,
14
,
13
,
30
,
33
]
). However, despite decades of intensive study, the existence of delocalized states remains unproven.

A prominent “toy model” for the random Schrödinger operator is the random band matrix. These matrices are characterized by the fact that the matrix elements
H_{ij}
become negligible when the distance between lattice points
i
and
j
exceeds a parameter
W
, known as the
band width
. Here,
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{d}
are lattice points in a
d
-dimensional space. Random band matrices play an important role in random matrix theory, and they serve as local models when
W
is small, gradually transitioning to the standard Wigner matrices as
W
approaches
N
. As
W
varies from order one to
N
, these matrices interpolate between local models and mean-field models, represented by Wigner matrices.

Band matrices can be real or complex, with typically no fundamental differences between them. However, complex band matrices are often easier to analyze due to simpler diagrammatic methods. Thus, we focus on complex Hermitian band matrices in this paper.

In the special case of
d=1
, it was conjectured
[
10
,
9
,
26
]
and supported by a nonrigorous supersymmetry method
[
29
]
that the eigenvectors of band matrices undergo a localization-delocalization transition, accompanied by a corresponding transition in the eigenvalue distribution.
Specifically, the conjecture suggests:

(i)

For
W\gg\sqrt{N}
, the bulk eigenvectors are delocalized, and the eigenvalue statistics follow the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE).

(ii)

For
W\ll\sqrt{N}
, the bulk eigenvectors are localized, and the eigenvalue statistics resemble a Poisson point process.

There are similar statements for the edge cases, with the transition occurring at
W=N^{5/6}
. While the bulk transition has not yet been fully established, the edge cases were solved in various models of band matrices
[
44
]
by Sodin, using moment methods. Although the conjectured transition at
W=\sqrt{N}
in the bulk remains open, there are several partial results
[
2
,
12
,
11
,
25
,
20
,
18
,
31
,
6
,
5
,
4
,
51
,
36
,
34
,
44
,
38
,
41
,
43
,
39
,
42
]
, but the localization-delocalization transition in one dimension is still a fundamental problem.

In the case where the covariance of the Gaussian matrix elements follows a specific profile, supersymmetric methods can be applied
[
2
,
38
,
41
,
43
,
40
,
15
]
(see
[
17
,
47
]
for overviews). With this method, for
d=3
, precise estimates on the density of states
[
15
]
were first obtained. A transition at
W=N^{1/2}
was established in
[
38
,
41
]
for the moments of characteristic polynomials, while a more challenging result regarding the two-point functions was proven in
[
40
]
by Shcherbina and Shcherbina.

There have been partial results on delocalization in dimensions
d>1
. In particular, delocalization and quantum diffusion were established in dimensions
d\geq 7

[
49
,
50
,
48
]
, using complex graphic expansion methods. Despite the complicated nature of these expansions, they introduced crucial concepts such as the sum-zero property of the self-energy, which will play an important role in our analysis.

A fundamental quantity in all these works concerning resolvent estimates of band matrices is the
T
-observable, introduced in
[
24
]
,

(1.1)

\displaystyle T_{xy}\sim\sum_{a}S_{xa}|G_{ay}|^{2},\quad S_{xa}=\mathbb{E}|H_{xa}|^{2}.

where
H
is the band matrix. The
T
-observable was analyzed in details in
[
49
,
50
,
48
]
via diagramtic method.

Recently, a sufficient condition for delocalization in terms of
W
was improved in
[
16
]
. Dubova and Yang utilized a time-dependent approach (previously applied to Wigner matrices
[
46
,
45
]
by Sooster and Warzel) and a linearization of the stochastic flow of the
T
-observable. Recall the standard complex matrix Brownian motion:

dH_{t,ij}=\sqrt{S_{ij}}dB_{t,ij},\quad H_{0}=0,

where
B_{t,ij}
are standard independent complex Brownian motions for all
i\leq j=1,\dots,N
, and
B_{ji}=\overline{B}_{ij}
for all
i,j
. Following
[
46
,
45
,
16
]
, we consider the Green’s function
H_{t}
with a time-dependent spectral parameter
z_{t}
such that the dynamics of

G_{t}^{(E)}:=(H_{t}-z_{t})^{-1}

are naturally renormalized up to the leading order. Under this flow, one can easily derive an equation for the
T
-observable, which unfortunately depends on higher-order objects.

A nature class to consider is the generalized
T
-observables defined by

(1.2)

\displaystyle T_{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}}=\sum_{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}S_{x_{i}a_{i}}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}G_{a_{i}a_{i+1}}(z_{i}),\quad a_{n+1}:=a_{1},\quad z_{i}\in\{z,\overline{z}\}.

for all
n
. On the other hand, estimating these quantities seems to be even more daunting than estimating
T
.
A nature question arises regarding the sizes of the generalized
T
-observables.
Since
\sum_{s}S_{ab}=1
, one might expect the naive bound

T_{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}}\sim(\max_{x\neq y}|G_{xy}(z)|)^{n}.

It turns out that their true sizes are

T_{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}}\sim(\max_{x\neq y}|G_{xy}(z)|)^{2n-2}.

In this paper, we will work on the following
G
-loop observable instead of generalized
T
-observables for some technical convenience, i.e.,

{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\big\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}G_{t}(\sigma_{i})\cdot E_{a_{i}}\big\rangle,\quad\text{where}\quad\left\langle A\right\rangle=\text{Tr}A.

The
G
-loops satisfy a system of evolution equations called the loop hierarchy (
2.45
). The dynamics of an
n
-loop depend on the
n+1
-loop and a martingale term, whose quadratic variation depends on
2n+2
loops.

The absence of a closed equation for the
n
-loop is a common feature in many-body dynamics, similar to the well-known BBGKY hierarchy in classical mechanics, which governs
n
-point correlation functions. Analyzing such hierarchies often requires truncation, but estimating errors due to truncation in higher correlation functions has proven difficult. This is why no powerful rigorous analysis of the BBGKY hierarchy has been available despite its introduction over a century ago.

A main contribution of
[
16
]
was to provide a controlled truncation of the
T
-observable dynamics. In this paper, we instead approximate the loop hierarchy for arbitrary length by introducing the
primitive hierarchy
, consisting only of the quadratic terms on the right-hand side of the loop hierarchy. Despite the nonlinear nature of the primitive hierarchy, it turns out that there is an explicit expression, the primitive loops, solving this hierarchy exactly. Furthermore, we will show that the primitive loops are excellent approximations to the
G
-loops.

It is insightful to compare the current approach with previous work on high-dimensional band matrices
[
49
,
48
]
. While the results in higher dimensions closely parallel those presented here, the methods differ significantly. The high-dimensional analysis relies on intricate graphical expansions derived via integration by parts, whereas our approach centers on the loop hierarchy—a stochastic dynamical structure. Despite this methodological divergence, a crucial feature identified in the high-dimensional setting—the sum-zero property—plays a major role in our analysis of the primitive hierarchy in Section
3
.

By analyzing the loop hierarchy, we will prove the delocalization of one dimensional band matrices for
W\gg\sqrt{N}
. Additionally, we will establish the accompanying quantum diffusion, quantum unique ergodicity and universality of local eigenvalue statistics. We believe that the loop hierarchy method introduced in this paper can be extended to other band matrices, including models with general variance profiles and higher dimensions, and random Schrödinger equations with blocked random potentials. We plan to address these issues in future works.

2
The model and main results

2.1
Band matrix model

In this paper, we focus on the band block matrix model, which is defined as follows. Although our methods can be extended to a larger class of band matrices, this specific model allows us to avoid many technical complications that are not central to the main results.
The model is described by a complex complex Hermitian random band matrix
H
whose entries are independent complex Gaussian random variables (up to the Hermitian condition
H_{ij}=\overline{H_{ji}}
) such that

H_{ij}\sim\mathcal{CN}(0,S_{ij}),\quad i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{N},

where
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
denotes the set of integers modulo
N
, with periodic boundary conditions.
The matrix
H
has block structure, where the block size is given by an integer
W\in\mathbb{N}
, and the number of blocks is denoted by
L\in\mathbb{N}
. The total matrix size is
N\times N
, with

N=W\cdot L.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{a}
denote the interval

\mathcal{I}_{a}:=\left[(a-1)W+1,\;aW\right],\quad a\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}.

The elements of the matrix
S
are defined by

S_{ij}=\frac{1}{3W}\sum_{a}\mathbf{1}(i\in\mathcal{I}_{a})\mathbf{1}(j\in\mathcal{I}_{a}\cup\mathcal{I}_{a+1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{a-1}),

where
\mathbf{1}(\cdot)
denotes the indicator function. Clearly,
S=S^{T}
, and it satisfies

\sum_{j}S_{ij}=1.

We will follow the convention that indices
a,b,c,\dots
are elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{L}
, while
i,j,k,\dots
represent indices in
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
. We can express the matrix
S
as a Kronecker product:

S=S^{(B)}\otimes S_{W},

where
S^{(B)}
is an
L\times L
matrix, and
S_{W}
is a
W\times W
matrix. The entries of these matrices are given by

S^{(B)}_{ab}=\frac{\mathbf{1}}{3}(|a-b|\leq 1),\quad(S_{W})_{ij}=W^{-1},\quad a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{L},\quad i,j\in\{1,\dots,W\}.

Let
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{N}
be the eigenvalues of
H
. Denote by
\left(\psi_{k}\right)_{k=1}^{N}
the corresponding normalized eigenvectors so that

H\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}=\lambda_{k}\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k},\quad\quad k=1,2\ldots N.

It is well known that the empirical spectral measure

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{\lambda_{k}}

converges almost surely to the Wigner semicircle law with density

\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{(4-x^{2})_{+}}.

Moreover, it is believed that the resolvent of
H
, i.e.,
(H-z)^{-1}
, exhibits quantum diffusion, meaning that

\mathbb{E}\left|(H-z)^{-1}\right|_{xy}^{2}\sim\left(\frac{|m|^{2}}{1-|m|^{2}S}\right)_{xy},

where
m=m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of the semicircle density
\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}
, defined by

m(z):=m_{\mathrm{sc}}(z):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)}{x-z}\,dx.

Using the definitions of
S
,
S^{(B)}
, and
S_{W}
, we can express

(1-|m|^{2}\cdot S)^{-1}=I+\frac{|m|^{2}S^{(B)}}{1-|m|^{2}\cdot S^{(B)}}\otimes S_{W}.

We define the diffusion length at the block level by

(2.1)

\ell(z):=\min\left(\eta^{-1/2},L\right)+1,\quad\eta=\mathrm{Im}(z).

In the following Lemma
2.14
, we show that
S^{(B)}
decays exponentially with scale
\ell(z)
:

\left(\frac{1}{1-|m|^{2}S^{(B)}}\right)_{ab}=O\left((\eta\ell)^{-1}\cdot e^{-c\frac{|a-b|}{\ell}}\right),

where
c
is a constant.

Stochastic Domination

In this paper, we adopt the convention of stochastic domination introduced in
[
19
]
. This framework will be used throughout the analysis to control the behavior of random matrices and their spectral properties.

Definition 2.1

(Stochastic domination and high probability event)
.

(i)
Let

\xi=\left(\xi^{(N)}(u):N\in\mathbb{N},u\in U^{(N)}\right),\quad\zeta=\left(\zeta^{(N)}(u):N\in\mathbb{N},u\in U^{(N)}\right),

be two families of non-negative random variables, where
U^{(N)}
is a possibly
N
-dependent parameter set. We say
\xi
is stochastically dominated by
\zeta
, uniformly in
u
, if for any fixed (small)
\tau>0
and (large)
D>0
,

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\bigcup_{u\in U^{(N)}}\left\{\xi^{(N)}(u)>N^{\tau}\zeta^{(N)}(u)\right\}\bigg)\leq N^{-D}

for large enough
N\geq N_{0}(\tau,D)
, and we will use the notation
\xi\prec\zeta
.
If for some complex family
\xi
we have
|\xi|\prec\zeta
, then we will also write
\xi\prec\zeta
or
\xi=\OO_{\prec}(\zeta)
.

(ii)
As a convention, for two deterministic non-negative quantities
\xi
and
\zeta
, we will write
\xi\prec\zeta
if and only if
\xi\leq N^{\tau}\zeta
for any constant
\tau>0
.

(iii)
Let
A
be a family of random matrices and
\zeta
be a family of non-negative random variables. Then, we use
A=\OO_{\prec}(\zeta)
to mean that
\|A\|\prec\xi
, where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the operator norm.

(iv)
We say an event
\Xi
holds with high probability (w.h.p.) if for any constant
D>0
,
\mathbb{P}(\Xi)\geq 1-N^{-D}
for large enough
N
. More generally, we say an event
\Omega
holds
w.h.p.
in
\Xi
if for any constant
D>0
,

\mathbb{P}(\Xi\setminus\Omega)\leq N^{-D}
for large enough
N
.

2.2
Main results

The following theorems are our main results on delocalization, local semicircle law, quantum unique ergodicity and quantumn diffusion.

Theorem 2.2

(Delocalizaiton)
.

Suppose that for some
\mathfrak{c}>0
,

(2.2)

W\geq N^{1/2\,+\,\mathfrak{c}}.

For band matrix defined in this subsection we have the following estimate.
For any (small) constants
\kappa,\tau>0
and (large)
D>0
, there exists
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{k}\left\|\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}\right\|_{\infty}^{2}\cdot{\bf 1}\big(\lambda_{k}\in[-2+\kappa,2-\kappa]\big)\leqslant N^{-1+\tau}\right)\geqslant 1-N^{-D}

Theorem 2.3

(Local semicircle law)
.

Suppose that the assumption of Theorem
2.2
holds. We denote the Green’s function by

G(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
.
Then for
any fixed small constants
\kappa,\tau>0
, large
D>0
, and

z=E+i\eta,\quad\quad|E|\leq 2-\kappa,\quad 1\geq\eta\geq N^{-1+\tau},

there exists
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
we have the local law (with
\ell=\ell(z)
defined in (
2.1
))

(2.3)

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}}\left|\left(G(z)-m(z)\right)_{xy}\right|\leq\frac{W^{\tau}}{(W\ell\eta)^{1/2}}\cdot\right)\geqslant 1-N^{-D},\quad\ell=\ell(z)

and the partial tracial local law

(2.4)

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{a}\Big|W^{-1}\sum_{x\in{\cal I}_{a}}G_{xx}(z)-m(z)\Big|\leq\frac{W^{\tau}}{W\ell\eta}\right)\geqslant 1-N^{-D},\quad\ell=\ell(z).

The partial tracial local law implies the standard tracial local law

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{a}\Big|N^{-1}\tr G(z)-m(z)\Big|\leq\frac{W^{\tau}}{W\ell\eta}\right)\geqslant 1-N^{-D},\quad\ell=\ell(z).

Theorem 2.4

(Quantum diffusion)
.

Denote by
E_{a}
the block identity matrix

(2.5)

(E_{a})_{ij}=\delta_{ij}\cdot W^{-1}\cdot\textbf{1}(i\in{\cal I}_{a}),\quad 1\leq i,j\leq N

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.3
and the notation
m=m(z)
we have

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{a,b}\left|\tr GE_{a}G^{\dagger}E_{b}-W^{-1}\left(\frac{|m|^{2}}{1-|m|^{2}S^{(B)}}\right)_{ab}\right|\leq\frac{W^{\tau}}{\left(W\ell\eta\right)^{2}}\right)\geq 1-N^{-D},

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{a,b}\left|\tr GE_{a}GE_{b}-W^{-1}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{1-m^{2}S^{(B)}}\right)_{ab}\right|\leq\frac{W^{\tau}}{\left(W\ell\eta\right)^{2}}\right)\geq 1-N^{-D},

and a stronger bound on the expectation value

(2.8)

\displaystyle\max_{a,b}\left|\mathbb{E}\tr GE_{a}G^{\dagger}E_{b}-W^{-1}\left(\frac{|m|^{2}}{1-|m|^{2}S^{(B)}}\right)_{ab}\right|\leq\left(W\ell\eta\right)^{-3}\cdot W^{\tau},

(2.9)

\displaystyle\max_{a,b}\left|\mathbb{E}\tr GE_{a}GE_{b}-W^{-1}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{1-m^{2}S^{(B)}}\right)_{ab}\right|\leq\left(W\ell\eta\right)^{-3}\cdot W^{\tau}.

Theorems
2.2
and
2.5
are simple consequences of Theorems
2.3
and
2.4
.

Proof of Theorem
2.2
.

Following a standard delocalization argument, we have

(2.10)

|\psi_{k}(x)|^{2}\leq\sum_{l}\frac{\eta^{2}|\psi_{l}(x)|^{2}}{(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{l})^{2}+\eta^{2}}\leq\eta\im G_{xx}(\lambda_{k}+\eta i)

By assumption (
2.2
),
\eta=N^{-1+\tau}
with small enough
\tau>0
satisfying

\eta\leq(W/N)^{2}
.
With this choice of
\eta
, we have
\ell\sim L
. Applying (
2.3
) and the fact that
\ell\sim L
, we obtain
G-m\ll 1
and thus
G=O(1)
with high probability. Inserting it back to (
2.10
), we obtain

|\psi_{k}(x)|^{2}\leq C\eta\leq N^{-1+\tau}

with high probability. This completes the proof of Theorem
2.2
.
∎

Quantum diffusion immediately implies the following probabilistic
generalized quantum unique ergodicity in high probability. This notion is an extension of the probabilistic
generalized quantum unique ergodicity first proved by
[
3
]
in the setting of Wigner matrices. Notice that a deterministic version of quantum unique ergodicity was a central concept in the work by
[
35
]
.

Theorem 2.5

(Generalized quantum unique ergodicity)
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
2.2
hold and

(2.11)

\displaystyle 0<\tau^{*}<\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2},

where
\mathfrak{c}
was defined in (
2.2
).
Then generalized quantum unique holds in high probability, i.e., for any small constant
\kappa>0
and large
D>0
, there exists a large
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geqslant N_{0}
,

(2.12)

\max_{E:\,|E|<2-\kappa}\;\max_{a\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}_{L}}\;\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i,j\in{\cal J}_{E}}\left|N(\psi_{i}^{*}\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}\right)\psi_{j})\right|^{2}\geq N^{-\tau^{*}/6}\right)\leq N^{-\tau^{*}/6}.

Here

{\cal J}_{E}:=\left\{x:|x-E|\leq N^{-1-\tau^{*}}(W^{2}/N)^{1/3}\right\}.

Furthermore, for any subset
A\subset\mathbb{Z}_{L}
,

(2.13)

\max_{E:\,|E|<2-\kappa}\;\max_{A\,\subset\,\mathbb{Z}_{L}}\;\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{k\in{\cal J}_{E}}\left|\,\sum_{x\in{\cal I}_{a}}\sum_{a\,\in\,A}\left|\boldsymbol{\psi}_{k}(x)\right|^{2}-\frac{|A|\cdot W}{N}\right|\geqslant\frac{|A|\cdot W}{N^{1+\tau^{*}/6}}\right)\leq N^{-\tau^{*}/6}

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

We first choose
z=E+\eta i,\quad\eta=N^{-1-\tau^{*}}(W^{2}/N)^{1/3}
.
By definition of
{\cal J}_{E}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}\textbf{1}(\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}\in{\cal J}_{E})\cdot\left|(\psi_{i}^{*}\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}\right)\psi_{j})\right|^{2}

(2.14)

\displaystyle\leq C\eta^{4}\sum_{i,j}\frac{\left|(\psi_{i}^{*}\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}\right)\psi_{j})\right|^{2}}{\left|\lambda_{i}-z\right|^{2}\left|\lambda_{j}-z\right|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\eta^{2}\tr\left(\operatorname{Im}G(z)\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}\right)\operatorname{Im}G(z)\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}\right)\right)

We can bound the last term by

(2.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\tr\left(\operatorname{Im}G(z)\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}\right)\operatorname{Im}G(z)\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\tr\left(\operatorname{Im}G(z)\left(E_{a}-E_{b}\right)\operatorname{Im}G(z)\left(E_{a}-E_{b^{\prime}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq C

\displaystyle\max_{a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\Big|\mathbb{E}\tr\left(\operatorname{Im}G(z)E_{a}\operatorname{Im}G(z)E_{b}\right)-\mathbb{E}\tr\left(\operatorname{Im}G(z)E_{a^{\prime}}\operatorname{Im}G(z)E_{b^{\prime}}\right)\Big|

By (
2.11
), we have
\eta^{-1/2}\geq L
and hence
\ell(z)=L
.
Then from (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) in Theorem
2.4
,
we have

\displaystyle\eta^{2}\mathbb{E}\tr\left(\operatorname{Im}G(z)\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}\right)\operatorname{Im}G(z)\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\eta^{2}(W\ell\eta)^{-3}\cdot W^{\delta}+\eta^{2}W^{-1+\delta}\max_{a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\;\max_{\xi=|m|^{2}\;\rm or\;m^{2}}\left|\left(\frac{1}{1-\xi\cdot S^{(B)}}\right)_{ab}-\left(\frac{1}{1-\xi\cdot S^{(B)}}\right)_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle W^{\delta}N^{-2}\big[N^{-1}\eta^{-1}+N^{2}\eta^{3/2}W^{-1}\big]=W^{\delta}N^{-2}\big[N^{\tau^{*}-2\mathfrak{c}/3}+N^{-3\tau^{*}/2}\big]<N^{-2}N^{-\tau^{*}/3},

provided that
\delta
is small enough and (
2.11
) is satisfied. Here we have used the
\ell(z)=L
,

(2.16)

\displaystyle|m(z)|^{2}\leq 1-c\,\eta,

and the estimate on
(1-|m|^{2}S^{(B)})^{-1}
in Lemma
2.14
. Inserting the above estimate back to the right hand side of (
2.2
), we have

(2.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\;\eta^{4}\sum_{i,j}\frac{\left|N(\psi_{i}^{*}\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}\right)\psi_{j})\right|^{2}}{\left|\lambda_{i}-z\right|^{2}\left|\lambda_{j}-z\right|^{2}}\leq N^{-\tau^{*}/3}.

Therefore with probability
1-O(N^{-\tau^{*}/6})
, the right hand side of (
2.2
) is bounded by
N^{-\tau^{*}/6}
. Together with (
2.2
), we obtain (
2.12
). For (
2.13
), we only need to replace
E_{a}
in (
2.2
) with
|A|^{-1}\sum_{a\in A}E_{a}
and use the same argument. This completes the proof Theorem
2.5

∎

2.3
Universality

Recall that the
k
-point correlation functions of
H
are defined by

\rho_{H}^{(k)}\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}\right):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N-k}}\rho_{H}^{(N)}\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{N}\right)\mathrm{d}\alpha_{k+1}\cdots\mathrm{~d}\alpha_{N},

where
\rho_{H}^{(N)}\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{N}\right)
is the joint density of all unordered eigenvalues of
H
.

Theorem 2.6

(Bulk universality)
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
2.2
holds. For any fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
point correlation function of
H
converges to that of GUE in the following sense. For any
|E|\leq 2-\kappa
and smooth test function
\mathcal{O}
with compact support, we have

(2.18)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\mathrm{~d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\left\{\left(\rho_{H}^{(k)}-\rho_{\mathrm{GUE}}^{(k)}\right)\left(E+\frac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{N}\right)\right\}=0

This universality result demonstrates that the local eigenvalue statistics at a fixed energy level for band matrices match those of Wigner matrices. However, the distributions of individual eigenvalues of band matrices differ significantly from those of Wigner matrices. As suggested by Theorem 1 of
[
37
]
, the fluctuations of bulk eigenvalues in band matrices are of order at least
(WN)^{-1/2}
, whereas the fluctuations for the eigenvalues of Wigner matrices are of order
N^{-1}

[
23
]
.

Proof of Theorem
2.6
.

Our proof adopts the strategy employed in Theorem 1.3 of
[
48
]
, which establishes the bulk universality of certain high-dimensional band matrices. In
[
48
]
, it is demonstrated in section 1.2 of
[
48
]
that universality follows from the local law (Theorem
2.3
in our setting),
delocalization (Theorem
2.2
)
and QUE estimates (Theorem
2.5
).

To compare the correlation functions of band matrix
H
and the GUE random matrix
H_{GUE}
, define the matrix Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process
H_{t}
as the solution to

(2.19)

\mathrm{d}H_{t}=-\frac{1}{2}H_{t}\mathrm{~d}t+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\mathrm{~d}B_{t},\quad\text{ with }H_{0}=H.

By definition,
H_{\infty}=H_{GUE}
. We aim to show that

(2.20)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\mathrm{~d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\left\{\left(\rho_{H_{0}}^{(k)}-\rho_{H_{\infty}}^{(k)}\right)\left(E+\frac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{N}\right)\right\}=0

Step 1
: Using the local semicircle law (Theorem
2.3
) as input and applying Theorem 2.2 from
[
32
]
, we establish the universality of the correlation functions of
H_{t_{*}}
at
t_{*}=N^{-1+\tau_{*}}
for any fixed
\tau_{*}>0
, explicitly,

(2.21)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\left\{\left(\rho_{H_{t_{*}}}^{(k)}-\rho_{H_{\infty}}^{(k)}\right)\left(E+\frac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{N}\right)\right\}=0,\quad t_{*}=N^{-1+\tau_{*}}.

Step 2
: Analogously to Proposition 4.17 in
[
48
]
, we assert that there exists a constant
c^{\prime}>0
such that the following holds:
Fix a sufficiently small parameter
\tau_{U}>0
(temporarily denoted
\tau_{U}
for clarity in our universality proof) and assume
|E|\leq 2-\kappa
. Define complex parameters
z_{i}=E_{i}+\mathrm{i}\eta_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
, satisfying

(2.22)

|E_{i}-E|\leq CN^{-1},\quad N^{-1-\tau_{U}}\leq\eta_{i}\leq N^{-1+\tau_{U}},\quad t_{U}:=N^{-1+\tau_{U}}.

Under the conditions of Theorem
2.6
, we claim that for any fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
c^{\prime}>0
such that for sufficiently small
\tau_{U}
,

(2.23)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Im}m_{0}(z_{i})-\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Im}m_{t_{U}}(z_{i})\right|\leq N^{-c^{\prime}+C_{n}\tau_{U}},\quad t_{U}=N^{-1+\tau_{U}},

where

m_{t}(z_{i})=\frac{1}{N}\operatorname{tr}(H_{t}-z_{i})^{-1},\quad t\in[0,t_{U}].

Following the approach in
[
48
]
, for each fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we choose
\tau_{U}
to be much smaller than
c^{\prime}
. By applying the standard correlation function comparison theorem (Theorem 15.3 in
[
21
]
) and Proposition 4.17 from
[
48
]
, we conclude that for every fixed integer
k
, there exists a sufficiently small
\tau_{U}>0
such that

(2.24)

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\,\mathcal{O}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\left\{\left(\rho_{H_{0}}^{(k)}-\rho_{H_{t_{U}}}^{(k)}\right)\left(E+\frac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{N}\right)\right\}=0,\quad t_{U}=N^{-1+\tau_{U}}.

Combining (
2.24
) and (
2.21
) (choosing
t_{*}=t_{U}
), we obtain (
2.20
), which yields the universality result (
2.18
). The proof of our key claim (
2.23
) proceeds in the following steps.

Step 3
: By Lemma 4.18 of
[
48
]
we have for any fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
that

(2.25)

\displaystyle\;\left|\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Im}m_{0}\left(z_{i}\right)-\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Im}m_{t_{U}}\left(z_{i}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\;C\cdot t_{U}\cdot\max_{0\leq t\leq t_{U}}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u}L_{1,t}\left(z_{u}\right)\prod_{i\neq u}\operatorname{Im}m_{t}\left(z_{i}\right)+\sum_{u\neq v}L_{2,t}\left(z_{u},z_{v}\right)\prod_{i\neq u,v}\operatorname{Im}m_{t}\left(z_{i}\right)\right],

where
C>0
is an absolute constant and

\displaystyle L_{1,t}(z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{2}\in\left\{G_{t},G_{t}^{*}\right\}}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{a,b}\left(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{2}(z)\right)_{aa}S_{ab}^{\circ}\left(\mathbf{G}_{2}(z)\right)_{bb}\right|,

\displaystyle L_{2,t}\left(z_{1},z_{2}\right)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{2}\in\left\{G_{t},G_{t}^{*}\right\}}\left|\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{a,b}\left(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{2}\left(z_{1}\right)\right)_{ab}S_{ab}^{\circ}\left(\mathbf{G}_{2}^{2}\left(z_{2}\right)\right)_{ba}\right|,

S_{xy}^{\circ}=S_{xy}-N^{-1}.

Similar to
[
48
]
, we claim that the following weaker versions of Theorems
2.3
and
2.5
hold for
H_{t}
defined in (
2.19
).

1.

(Weak version of Theorem
2.3
): Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.2
, for sufficiently small
\tau_{U}
(depending on
\mathfrak{c}
in (
2.2
)), and parameters satisfying

t\in[0,t_{U}],\quad t_{U}=N^{-1+\tau_{U}},\quad z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta,\quad|E|<2-\kappa,\quad\eta=N^{-1+2\tau_{U}},

we have

(2.26)

\max_{x}|(G_{t})_{xx}|\prec 1,\quad G_{t}:=(H_{t}-z)^{-1}.

(Note: Here, we explicitly state and prove only the case
\eta=N^{-1+4\tau_{U}}
, although the general case should also hold.)

2.

(Weak version of Theorem
2.5
): For sufficiently small fixed
\tau_{U}
(depending on
\mathfrak{c}
in (
2.2
)), we have

(2.27)

Theorem
2.5
holds for
H_{t}
, with
\tau^{*}=\mathfrak{c}/3
, and
0\leq t\leq N^{-1+\tau_{U}}
.

We remark that the results above remain valid without the extra condition on
\eta
. However, this simplified form is sufficient for our universality proof and significantly streamlines the argument.

We postpone the proof of (
2.26
) and (
2.27
) to subsection
7.2
.
Assuming these two results, we complete the proof of of Theorem
2.6
.

It is well-known that for any
E
in the bulk that

\eta\leq\widetilde{\eta}\implies\eta\operatorname{Im}m\left(E+\mathrm{i}\eta\right)\leq\tilde{\eta}\operatorname{Im}m\left(E+\mathrm{i}\tilde{\eta}\right).

By choosing
\widetilde{\eta}=N^{-1+2\tau_{U}}
and (
2.26
), we obtain that for
z_{i}
’s in (
2.22
),

\max_{0\leq t\leq t_{U}}\im m_{t}(z_{i})\prec N^{2\tau_{U}}\cdot(N\im z_{i})^{-1}\prec N^{3\tau_{U}}.

Inserting it back to (
2.25
), we obtain that

(2.28)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Im}m_{0}\left(z_{i}\right)-\mathbb{E}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Im}m_{t_{U}}\left(z_{i}\right)\right|\;\prec\;N^{-1+C_{n}\tau_{U}}\cdot\max_{0\leq t\leq t_{U}}\max_{u\neq v}\,\mathbb{E}\left[L_{1,t}\left(z_{u}\right)+L_{2,t}\left(z_{u},z_{v}\right)\right].

For bounding
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, in the following we will prove that, for
\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{2}\in\left\{G_{t},G_{t}^{*}\right\}
, and
z_{i}
’s in (
2.22
)

(2.29)

\displaystyle\max_{i}\max_{x,y}\;\mathbb{E}\left|\sum_{x}\left(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{2}(z_{i})\right)_{xx}S_{xy}^{\circ}\left(\mathbf{G}_{2}(z_{i})\right)_{yy}\right|\leq N^{1-\mathfrak{c}/18+C\tau_{U}}

(2.30)

\displaystyle\max_{i\neq j}\max_{x,y}\;\mathbb{E}\left|\sum_{x}\left(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{2}\left(z_{i}\right)\right)_{xy}S_{xy}^{\circ}\left(\mathbf{G}_{2}^{2}\left(z_{j}\right)\right)_{yx}\right|\prec N^{2-\mathfrak{c}/18+C\tau_{U}}.

By Lemma 4.20 of
[
48
]
(or use the eigen-decomposition
of
G
)

(2.31)

\left|\sum_{x}\left(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{2}(z)\right)_{xx}S_{xy}^{\circ}\left(\mathbf{G}_{2}(z)\right)_{yy}\right|\prec N^{-2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|p_{\alpha}(z)|^{2}\cdot|p_{\beta}(z)|\cdot\left|M_{y,\alpha}\right|,

where

p_{\alpha}(z):=\left(\lambda_{\alpha}-z\right)^{-1}

and

M_{y,\alpha}:=N\sum_{x}|u_{\alpha}(x)|^{2}S^{\circ}_{xy}=N\sum_{a:|a-a_{0}|\leq 1}\frac{1}{3}\left\langle u_{\alpha}\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}I\right)\,u^{*}_{\alpha}\right\rangle,\quad y\in{\cal I}_{a_{0}}.

With the local law estimate (
2.26
) at the scale
N^{-1+2\tau_{U}}
, and the eigenvector
L_{\infty}
bound in (
2.10
), one can obtain that

(2.32)

\max_{\alpha,y}|M_{y,\alpha}|\prec N^{C\tau_{U}},\quad\sum_{\alpha}|p_{\alpha}(z)|^{2}\prec N^{2+C\tau_{U}},\quad\sum_{\beta}|p_{\beta}(z)|\prec N^{1+C\tau_{U}}

On the other hand,
M_{y,\alpha}
has a sharper bound with high probability
1-o(1)
).
To see this, define the bad event
\cal B

{\cal B}=\left\{\exists\alpha\mbox{ such that }|\lambda_{\alpha}-E|\leq N^{-1+\mathfrak{c}/6}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left|M_{x,\alpha}\right|\geq N^{-\mathfrak{c}/18}\right\}.

Using the QUE estimate of
H_{t}
in (
2.27
) and (
2.12
) with
\tau^{*}=\mathfrak{c}/3
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left({\cal B}\right)=O\left(N^{-\mathfrak{c}/18}\right).

Therefore, by (
2.32
),

\mathbb{E}\;\left({\bf 1}_{{\cal B}}\cdot\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|p_{\alpha}(z)|^{2}\cdot|p_{\beta}(z)|\cdot\left|M_{x,\alpha}\right|\right)=O(N^{3-\mathfrak{c}/18+C\tau_{U}}).

On the good event
{\cal B}^{c}
, we split the sum
\alpha
into

\sum_{\alpha}=\sum^{\leq}_{\alpha}+\sum^{>}_{\alpha}\;,\quad\quad\sum^{\leq}_{\alpha}:=\sum_{\alpha:|\lambda_{\alpha}-E|\leq N^{-1+\mathfrak{c}/6}}.

Then

(2.33)

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{{\cal B}^{c}}\cdot\sum_{\alpha}^{\leq}|p_{\alpha}(z)|^{2}\cdot\left|M_{x,\alpha}\right|

\displaystyle\;\prec\;{\bf 1}_{{\cal B}^{c}}\cdot N^{-\mathfrak{c}/18}\cdot\sum_{\alpha}^{\leq}|p_{\alpha}(z)|^{2}\leq N^{2-\mathfrak{c}/18+C\tau_{U}}

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{{\cal B}^{c}}\cdot\sum_{\alpha}^{>}|p_{\alpha}(z)|^{2}\cdot\left|M_{x,\alpha}\right|

\displaystyle\;\prec\;{\bf 1}_{{\cal B}^{c}}\cdot N^{C\tau_{U}}\cdot\sum_{\alpha}^{>}|p_{\alpha}(z)|^{2}\leq N^{2-\mathfrak{c}/6+C\tau_{U}}.

Combining them with the last term in (
2.32
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left({\bf 1}_{{\cal B}^{c}}\cdot\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|p_{\alpha}(z)|^{2}\cdot|p_{\beta}(z)|\cdot\left|M_{x,\alpha}\right|\right)\prec N^{3-\mathfrak{c}/18+C\tau_{U}},

which implies (
2.29
).

To prove (
2.30
), we use Lemma 4.20 of
[
48
]
to have

\left|\sum_{x}\left(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{2}\left(z_{i}\right)\right)_{xy}S_{xy}^{\circ}\left(\mathbf{G}_{2}^{2}\left(z_{j}\right)\right)_{yx}\right|\prec N^{-2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|p_{\alpha}(z_{i})|^{2}|p_{\beta}(z_{j})|^{2}\left|M_{y,\alpha,\beta}\right|,

where

M_{y,\alpha,\beta}:=N\sum_{x}u_{\alpha}(x)\overline{u_{\beta}(x)}\cdot S^{0}_{xy}=\sum_{a:|a-a_{0}|\leq 1}\frac{N}{3}\left\langle u_{\alpha}\left(E_{a}-N^{-1}I\right)\,u^{*}_{\beta}\right\rangle,\quad y\in{\cal I}_{a_{0}}.

Similar to (
2.32
), we have

\left|M_{y,\alpha,\beta}\right|\prec N^{C\tau_{U}}.

Define the bad event

\widetilde{\cal B}:=\left\{\exists\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\mbox{ such that }\max_{i}|\lambda_{\alpha_{i}}-E|\leq N^{-1+\mathfrak{c}/6}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left|M_{y,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\right|\geq N^{-\mathfrak{c}/18}\right\}.

Again using (
2.27
) and (
2.12
) with
\tau^{*}=\mathfrak{c}/3
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{{\cal B}})=O\left(N^{-\mathfrak{c}/18}\right)
and

\mathbb{E}\;\left({\bf 1}_{\widetilde{{\cal B}}}\cdot\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|p_{\alpha}(z_{i})|^{2}|p_{\beta}(z_{j})|^{2}\left|M_{y,\alpha,\beta}\right|\right)=O(N^{4-\mathfrak{c}/18+C\tau_{U}}).

On the good set, we have

{\bf 1}_{\widetilde{{\cal B}}^{c}}\cdot\sum_{\alpha,\beta}|p_{\alpha}(z_{i})|^{2}|p_{\beta}(z_{j})|^{2}\left|M_{y,\alpha,\beta}\right|\leq N^{4-\mathfrak{c}/18+C\tau_{U}}.

We have thus proved (
2.30
).
Combining it with (
2.29
) and (
2.28
) (and the definitions of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
), we obtain (
2.23
) (with
c^{\prime}=\mathfrak{c}/18
).
This completes the proof of of Theorem
2.6
under the assumption that
(
2.27
) and (
2.26
) hold.

∎

2.4
Stochastic flow and
G
-loops

In this section, we state a fundamental estimate on “
G
loops” which will be the key to prove
Theorems
2.3
and
2.4
. To this end, recall
the matrix Brownian motion defined by

(2.34)

\displaystyle dH_{t,ij}=\sqrt{S_{ij}}dB_{t,ij},\quad H_{0}=0,

where
B_{t,ij}
are independent standard complex Brownian motions for all
i\leq j=1,\ldots,N
and
B_{ji}=\overline{B}_{ij}
for all
i,j
.
We will consider the resolvent with a time dependent spectral parameter
z_{t}
given by the following definition.

Definition 2.7

(The
z_{t}
flow)
.

For fixed
E\in\mathbb{R}
, denote by
m^{(E)}:=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0+}m_{sc}(E+i\epsilon)
.
Define the linear flow
z_{t}

(0\leq t\leq 1)
by

z^{(E)}_{t}=E+(1-t)m^{(E)},\quad 0\leq t\leq 1.

The imaginary part of
z^{(E)}_{t}
is given by

(2.35)

\displaystyle\eta_{t}=\im z^{(E)}_{t}=(1-t)\im m^{(E)}.

Denote the resolvent of
H_{t}
at
z^{(E)}_{t}
by

(2.36)

\displaystyle G_{t}^{(E)}:=(H_{t}-z^{(E)}_{t})^{-1}.

By Ito’s formula,
G_{t}:=G_{t}^{(E)}
satisfies the SDE

\displaystyle dG_{t}=-G_{t}dH_{t}G_{t}+G_{t}\{\mathcal{S}[G_{t}]-m^{(E)}\}G_{t}dt,

where
\mathcal{S}:M_{N}(\mathbb{C})\to M_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is the linear operator defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}[X]_{ij}:=\delta_{ij}\sum_{k=1}^{N}S_{ik}X_{kk}.

Notice that
G_{t}
depends on
E
and we will use
G^{(E)}_{t}
to emphasize the
E
dependence.
For any spectral parameter
z
, we are interested in the resolvent
G(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
. This function can be related to
G_{t}^{(E)}
by the following lemma.

Lemma 2.8
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
0<\im z\leq 1
and
|\re z|\leq 2-\kappa
for some
\kappa>0
, there exists
(E,t)
satisfying
0<t<1
and
|E|\leq 2-c\kappa
for some
c>0
such that

(2.37)

z=t^{-1/2}\cdot z^{(E)}_{t}.

Denote by
m^{(E)}
the solution of
m(m+E)=-1
. Then
E
and
t
are explicitly given by

E=-2\frac{\re m_{sc}(z)}{|m_{sc}(z)|},\quad t=m_{sc}(z)^{2}/(m^{(E)})^{2}.

For
z
,
E
and
t
satisfy (
2.37
), we have

(2.38)

m_{sc}(z)=t^{1/2}\cdot m^{(E)}

and

(2.39)

G(z)\sim t^{1/2}\cdot G_{t}^{(E)}

in the sense that they have the same distribution.
Furthermore, there exists
c_{\kappa}>0
(depending only on
\kappa
) such that

(2.40)

t\geq c_{\kappa}\quad{\rm and}\quad c_{\kappa}\im z\leq\im z_{t}\leq c_{\kappa}^{-1}\im z

Proof.

For (
2.37
),
we wish to solve

z=t^{-1/2}\big[(E+m^{(E)})-tm^{(E)}\big]=\frac{E+m^{(E)}}{\sqrt{t}}-\sqrt{t}m^{(E)}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}m^{(E)}}-\sqrt{t}m^{(E)}

This equation can be solved if we can find
t
and
m
so that
\sqrt{t}m^{(E)}=m_{sc}(z)
.
The last equation is solved by
\sqrt{t}=|m_{sc}(z)|
with
E
solving
m^{(E)}=m_{sc}(z)/|m_{sc}(z)|
. Explicitly,
E=-2\frac{\re m_{sc}(z)}{|m_{sc}(z)|}
. The other properties can be checked directly.
∎

For the rest of the paper, we will consider only
G_{t}^{(E)}
. We now define the
G
loops.

Definition 2.9

(
G
- Loop)
.

For fixed
E
and
\sigma\in\{+,-\}
, define

G^{(E)}_{t}(\alpha):=G^{(E)}_{t,\alpha}=\begin{cases}(H_{t}-z_{t})^{-1},\quad\alpha=+\\
(H_{t}-\overline{z}_{t})^{-1},\quad\alpha=-\end{cases}

By definition,
G^{(E)}_{t,+}=\left(G^{(E)}_{t,-}\right)^{\dagger}
.
For simplicity of notations, we sometimes write

G_{t}=G^{(E)}_{t,+}
which matches the notation in (
2.39
). Recall
E_{a}
defined in (
2.5
).
For

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\cdots\sigma_{n}),\quad\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots a_{n}),\quad\sigma_{i}\in\{+,-\},\quad a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L},\quad 1\leq i\leq n

we define the
n
-
G
loop by

(2.41)

{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\big\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}G_{t}(\sigma_{i})\cdot E_{a_{i}}\big\rangle,\quad\text{where}\quad\left\langle A\right\rangle=\tr A.

We also denote by

(2.42)

m(\sigma):=m^{(E)}(\sigma)=\begin{cases}m^{(E)},&\sigma=+\\
\overline{m^{(E)}},&\sigma=-\end{cases},

and

(2.43)

\widetilde{G}_{t}(\sigma_{k}):=G_{t}(\sigma_{k})-m(\sigma_{k}).

With these notations, we can express the quantity

\tr G(z)E_{a}G^{\dagger}(z)E_{b}
in Theorem
2.4
by
(we drop the superscript
E
)

\tr G(z)E_{a}G^{\dagger}(z)E_{b}=t\cdot\tr(G_{t,+})\cdot E_{a}\cdot(G_{t,-})\cdot E_{b}=t\cdot{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}.

In order to derive the loop hierarchy, Lemma
2.11
, we need the following notations.

Definition 2.10

(Loop, Cut, and Glue)
.

Recall the
G
loops
{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
defined in Eq. (
2.41
) of Definition
2.9
. Here we define some basic operators: cutting and gluing for these
G
loops. Assume that

(2.44)

{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}G_{t}(\sigma_{i})E_{a_{i}}\right\rangle,\quad\sigma_{i}\in\{+,-\},\quad 1\leq i\leq n

1.

For
1\leq k\leq n
, we define the first cut and glue operator
{\cal G}^{(a)}_{k}
such that
{\cal G}^{(a)}_{k}\circ{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
is the
G
loop obtained by replacing
G_{t}(\sigma_{k})
by
G_{t}(\sigma_{k})E_{a}G_{t}(\sigma_{k})
.
If we consider
{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
as a loop, then the operator
{\cal G}^{(a)}_{k}
cuts the
k
-th
G
edge
G_{t}(\sigma_{k})
and glues the two new ends with
E_{a}
.
We also view
{\cal G}_{k}^{(a)}
as an operator on the indices
\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}
so that

{\cal L}_{t,\;{\cal G}^{(a)}_{k}(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})}:={\cal G}^{(a)}_{k}\circ{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}.

The new loop is
{\cal G}^{(a)}_{k}\circ{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
one unit longer than the original
{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
. For example in figure
1
, for
n=4
:

{\cal G}^{(a)}_{2}\circ{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={\cal L}_{t,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime}},\quad\quad{\cal G}^{(a)}_{2}\circ(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime})

and

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4}),\quad\textbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}),\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4}),\quad\textbf{a}^{\prime}=(a_{1},a,a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})

a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}
G_{2}
G_{3}
G_{4}
G_{1}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}
a
G_{2}
G_{2}
G_{3}
G_{4}
G_{1}

Figure 1
:
Illustration of operator

{\cal G}^{(a)}_{k}

2.
For
1\leq k<l\leq n
, we define the cut and glue operator
{\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}
as follows:
{\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}\circ{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
(where
L
stands for ”left”) is the
G
loop obtained by cutting the
k
-th and
l
-th
G
edges
G_{t}(\sigma_{k})
and
G_{t}(\sigma_{l})
(creating four end points and two “chains”), then gluing the two new ends of the chain that contains
E_{a_{n}}
and inserting a new
E_{a}
at the gluing point. The length of the new loop will be
k+n-l+1
. For example in figure
2
, for
n=5
:

{\cal G}^{(a),L}_{3,5}\circ{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={\cal L}_{t,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime}},\quad{\cal G}^{(a),L}_{3,5}\circ\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}\right)=\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime}\right)

and

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4},\sigma_{5}),\quad\textbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4},a_{5}),\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{5}),\quad\textbf{a}^{\prime}=(a_{1},a_{2},a,a_{5})

Notice that
a_{n},a_{1}
are always in
{\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}
for any
k,l
.

:

{\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}

3.
For
1\leq k<l\leq n
, similarly, we define the cut and glue operator
{\cal G}^{(a),R}_{k,l}
(where
R
stands for ”right”) as
{\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}
. The difference is that this time we glue the two new ends of the chain that does not contain
E_{a_{n}}
. The length of the new loop will be
l-k+1
. For example in figure
3
for
n=5
:

{\cal G}^{(a),R}_{3,5}\circ{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={\cal L}_{t,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime}},\quad{\cal G}^{(a),R}_{3,5}\circ\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}\right)=\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime}\right)

and

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4},\sigma_{5}),\quad\textbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4},a_{5}),\quad\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}=(\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4},\sigma_{5}),\quad\textbf{a}^{\prime}=(a_{3},a_{4},a)

Figure 3
:
Illustration of operator
{\cal G}^{(a),R}_{k,l}

Notice that the loop containing the index
a_{n}
is the left loop, another one is right loop.

Denote by

\partial_{(i,j)}:=\partial_{H_{t}(i,j)},\quad 1\leq i\leq j\leq N
. By Itô’s formula, we have the following lemma.
We will use the convention that
\textbf{a}=(a_{1}\ldots a_{n})
is a vector while
a
will be used an index independent of
a
. We will use this convention throughout the paper.

Lemma 2.11

(The loop hierarchy)
.

The
G
-loops satisfy the loop hierarchy

(2.45)

\displaystyle d\mathcal{L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\mathcal{E}^{(\widetilde{G})}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+W\cdot\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sum_{a,b}\left(\mathcal{G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}\circ\mathcal{L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right)S^{(B)}_{ab}\left(\mathcal{G}^{(b),R}_{k,l}\circ\mathcal{L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right)dt,

where the martingale term and the
\widetilde{G}
terms are defined by

(2.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=(i,j)}\left(\partial_{\alpha}\;{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right)\cdot\left(S_{\alpha}\right)^{1/2}\cdot\mathrm{d}\left({\cal B}_{t}\right)_{\alpha}

(2.47)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\widetilde{G})}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}:=

\displaystyle{W}\cdot\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\sum_{a,b}\;\left\langle\widetilde{G}_{t}(\sigma_{k})E_{a}\right\rangle\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot\left({\cal G}^{(b)}_{k}\circ{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right)dt,\quad\quad\widetilde{G}_{t}=G_{t}-m

Notice that the factor
W
comes from that
E_{a}
has an
W^{-1}
factor.

In order to solve this hierarchy, we introduce the primitive loops, denoted by
\cal K
.

Definition 2.12

(The primitive equation)
.

For

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\cdots\sigma_{n}),\quad\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots a_{n}),\quad\sigma_{i}\in\{+,-\},\quad a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L},\quad 1\leq i\leq n

and
m(\sigma)
defined in (
2.42
), we define
{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
to be the unique solution to the equation

(2.48)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\,{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={W}\cdot\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sum_{a,b}\;\left({\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}\circ{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right)S^{(B)}_{ab}\left({\cal G}^{(b),R}_{k,l}\circ{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right)

with the initial value

{\cal K}_{0,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={W^{-n+1}}\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{n}m(\sigma_{k})\cdot\textbf{1}(a_{1}=a_{2}=\cdots=a_{n}).

Here we define the
{\cal G}
operator acting on
{\cal K}
in the same way as it acts on
{\cal L}
, i.e., ,

(2.49)

{\cal G}^{(a),\,L}_{k,l}\circ{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={\cal K}_{t,\;{\cal G}^{(a),\,L}_{k,l}(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})}\quad\forall t,k,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a},

and similarly for
{\cal G}^{(b),\,R}_{k,l}
.
For the special case
n=1
, we define
{\cal K}_{t,\,+,\,a}=m,\quad{\cal K}_{t,\,-,\,a}=\overline{m}
for
0\leq t\leq 1
.

Notice that
{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
is an
n
loop and
\partial_{\alpha}\;{\cal L}
is a product of
n+1
resolvent. Hence (
2.45
) is not an equation, but a hierarchy. However, the lengths of
{\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}\circ{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
and
{\cal G}^{(a),R}_{k,l}\circ{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
are no greater than the length of
{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
. Therefore,
the system of equations for
\cal K
can be solved inductively.
In the next section, we will provide an explicit solution to the primitive equation.

2.5
Propagator
\Theta_{\xi}^{(B)}
and examples of
{\cal K}

Definition 2.13

(Propagator
\Theta_{\xi}^{(B)}
)
.

Define the propagator
\Theta_{\xi}^{(B)}
by

(2.50)

\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi}:=\frac{1}{1-\xi\cdot S^{(B)}},\quad\xi\in\mathbb{C},\quad|\xi|<1

where the superscript
B
indicates the block level matrix. Clearly,

(2.51)

\partial_{\xi}\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi}=\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi}\cdot S^{(B)}\cdot\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi}.

By definition,
\Theta_{\xi}^{(B)}
is an
L\times L
matrices at the block level. We will
omit the subscript
(B)
for the rest of this paper. We remind the readers that
\Theta

was often used to denote the
N\times N
matrix

(1-\xi S)^{-1}
in the literature. In this paper,
\Theta_{\xi}=\Theta_{\xi}^{(B)}
.
The following three special cases are often used in this paper:

\Theta^{(B)}_{tm^{2}},\quad\Theta^{(B)}_{t\overline{m}^{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}}=\Theta^{(B)}_{t}.

where we have used
|m|=|m^{(E)}|=1
in our setting.

The following properties of
\Theta^{(B)}
can be easily verified.

Lemma 2.14
.

Suppose that
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
,
\im\,\xi>0
and
|\xi|\leq 1
. Define

\hat{\ell}(\xi):=\min\left((|1-\xi|)^{-1/2},\;L\right),\quad\xi\in\mathbb{C}

Then
\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi}
has the following properties:

1.

Symmetric:
(\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi})_{xy}=(\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi})_{yx}
.

2.

Translation invariant:
(\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi})_{xy}=(\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi})_{x+1,y+1}
.

3.

Commutativity:

\forall\;\xi,\;\xi^{\prime}\quad\quad[S^{(B)},\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi}]=[\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi\,^{\prime}},\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi}]=0

4.

Exponential decay at length scale
\hat{\ell}_{\xi}
:

(2.52)

(\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi})_{xy}\leq\frac{C\cdot e^{-c\cdot|x-y|\big/\hat{\ell}(\xi)}}{|1-\xi|\cdot\hat{\ell}(\xi)}\cdot\,,\quad\hat{\ell}(\xi):=\min\left((|1-\xi|)^{-1/2},\;L\right)

5.

The following random walk representation of
\Theta_{\xi}^{(B)}
converges for any
0\leq\xi<1
.

\displaystyle\Theta_{\xi}^{(B)}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\;\xi^{k}\cdot\left(S^{(B)}\right)^{k}

6.

Derivative bounds. Use above random walk representation, we have

(2.53)

\left|(\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi})_{x,y}-(\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi})_{x,{y+1}}\right|\prec\frac{1}{\hat{\ell}(\xi)\cdot|1-\xi|^{1/2}}

(2.54)

\left|2(\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi})_{x,y}-(\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi})_{x,{y+1}}-(\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi})_{x,{y-1}}\right|\prec\frac{1}{\|x-y\|+1}.

We can use
\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi}
to solve the primitive equation
\mathcal{K}_{t}
in the special cases
n=2
or
3
.

Example 2.15
.

For
n=2
, the primitive equation (
2.48
) can be written as

(2.55)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\,\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(a_{1},a_{2})}=W\cdot\sum_{a,b}\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(a_{1},a)}\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(b,a_{2})}

Denote

(2.56)

m_{i}=m(\sigma_{i}).

Using the propagator
\Theta^{(B)}
defined in equation (
2.50
) and the property (
2.51
), one can easily verify that

(2.57)

\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=W^{-1}m_{1}m_{2}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t\cdot m_{1}m_{2}}\right)_{a_{1}a_{2}},\quad\textbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2})

Explicitly, we have

(2.58)

{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(a,b)}=W^{-1}\cdot\begin{cases}|m|^{2}\left[\left(1-{t}|m|^{2}S^{(B)}\right)^{-1}\right]_{ab}&,\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(+,-)\\
m^{2}\left[\left(1-{t}m^{2}S^{(B)}\right)^{-1}\right]_{ab}&,\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(+,+)\end{cases}

Example 2.16
.

For
n=3
, let

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}),\,\textbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
.
Then (
2.48
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\,\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=

\displaystyle W\cdot\sum_{b_{1},c_{1}}\mathcal{K}_{t,(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}),(a_{1},b_{1})}\cdot S^{(B)}_{b_{1}c_{1}}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(c_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle W\cdot\sum_{b_{2},c_{2}}\mathcal{K}_{t,(\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}),(a_{2},b_{2})}\cdot S^{(B)}_{b_{2}c_{2}}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(a_{1},c_{2},a_{3})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle W\cdot\sum_{b_{3},c_{3}}\mathcal{K}_{t,(\sigma_{3},\sigma_{1}),(a_{3},b_{3})}\cdot S^{(B)}_{b_{3}c_{3}}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(a_{1},a_{2},c_{3})}

Using (
2.57
), we can rewrite it as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\,\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{c_{1}}\left(m_{1}m_{2}\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{1}m_{2}}\cdot S^{(B)}\right)_{a_{1}c_{1}}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(c_{1},a_{2},a_{3})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{c_{2}}\left(m_{2}m_{3}\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{2}m_{3}}\cdot S^{(B)}\right)_{a_{2}c_{2}}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(a_{1},c_{2},a_{3})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{c_{3}}\left(m_{3}m_{1}\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{3}m_{1}}\cdot S^{(B)}\right)_{a_{3}c_{3}}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(a_{1},a_{2},c_{3})}

Recall

m_{i}=m(\sigma_{i})
defined in (
2.56
). Using the propagator
\Theta^{(B)}
(
2.50
) and (
2.51
), one can easily verify that

{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\sum_{b}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t\cdot m_{1}m_{2}}\right)_{a_{1}b}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t\cdot m_{2}m_{3}}\right)_{a_{2}b}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t\cdot m_{3}m_{1}}\right)_{a_{3}b}\cdot W^{-2}\cdot m_{1}m_{2}m_{3},\quad\textbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}).

Alternatively, it can be expressed as

{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\sum_{b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t\cdot m_{1}m_{2}}\right)_{a_{1}b_{1}}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t\cdot m_{2}m_{3}}\right)_{a_{2}b_{2}}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t\cdot m_{3}m_{1}}\right)_{a_{3}b_{3}}\cdot{\cal K}_{0,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{b}},\quad\textbf{b}=(b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}).

In the next section, using the primitive equation and the tree representation of
\mathcal{K}
, we will establish the following estimate (
3.46
) for
\mathcal{K}
. A key ingredient in this proof is the sum-zero property, (
3.45
), which serves as a critical input.

Lemma 2.17

(An upper bound on
{\cal K}
)
.

Recall
\hat{\ell}
defined in (
2.52
).
The primitive loop
{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
is bounded by

(2.59)

{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\prec\left(W\ell_{t}\cdot\eta_{t}\right)^{-n+1},\quad\ell_{t}:=\hat{\ell}(t)=\min\left((|1-t|)^{-1/2},\;L\right)

Finally, we note that for any fixed
|E|<2-\kappa
, it is straightforward to verify that

\im z_{t}=\im z_{t}^{(E)}=\im m^{(E)}\cdot(1-t)\sim(1-t).

Therefore, the
\ell(z)
defined in (
2.1
) and
\ell_{t}
defined in (
2.59
) are of the same order, as follows:

\ell(z_{t})\sim\ell_{t}=\hat{\ell}(t).

Furthermore, for
z
,
t
, and
E
satisfying the conditions (
2.37
) and (
2.40
), we have

\im z\sim\im z^{(E)}_{t},\quad\ell(z)\sim\ell(z^{(E)}_{t})\sim\ell_{t}.

Notice that
\ell_{t}
is just a simpler notation for
\hat{\ell}(t)
.

2.6
Estimates on loops

Our main estimates for the
G
-loop are given in the following lemmas (Recall that
\prec
and
\ell_{t}
are defined in Definition
2.1
and (
2.59
) respectively).

Lemma 2.18

(loop estimates)
.

For any small constants
\kappa,\tau>0
and

E\in[-2+\kappa,2-\kappa],\,0\leq t\leq 1-N^{-1+\tau}
,
we have

(2.60)

\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\left|{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\prec(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-n},\quad\eta_{t}:=\im z_{t},

(2.61)

\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\left|{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\prec(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-n+1}.

Lemma 2.19

(
2
-loop estimate)
.

With the notations and assumptions of the previous lemma,
the expectation of
2
-
G
-loop is bounded by

(2.62)

\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\left|\mathbb{E}{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\prec(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-3},\quad\eta_{t}:=\im z_{t}

for any choice of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\{+,-\}^{2}
.
In case
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(+,-)
and
\textbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2})
,
we have

(2.63)

\left|{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\prec(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-2}\exp\left(-\left|\frac{a_{1}-a_{2}}{\ell_{t}}\right|^{1/2}\right)+W^{-D}.

Lemma 2.20

(Local law for
G_{t}
)
.

With the notations and assumptions of the previous lemma, we have

(2.64)

\|G^{(E)}_{t,+}-m^{(E)}\|_{\max}\prec(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-1/2}.

Proof of Theorems
2.3
and
2.4
.

For each
z
in Theorems
2.3
and
2.4
, Lemma
2.8
shows that there exist
E
and
t
satisfying (
2.37
) and (
2.40
), along with the following conditions:

(1-t)\sim\im z_{t}\sim\im z\geq N^{-1+\tau},\quad\eta_{t}\sim\eta,\quad\ell_{t}\sim\ell(z).

Combining (
2.38
) and (
2.39
), we obtain

(2.65)

G(z)-m_{sc}(z)=t^{1/2}\left(G_{t}^{(E)}-m^{(E)}\right).

Thus, the estimate on
G-m
in (
2.3
) of Theorem
2.3
follows from the estimate on
G_{t}-m
in (
2.64
) of Lemma
2.20
.

Similarly, (
2.4
) follows from (
2.60
) for the
1
-
G
-loop, with the definition of
\cal K
in Definition
2.12
:

{\cal K}_{t,+,a}=m^{(E)},\quad 0\leq t\leq 1.

Analogously to (
2.65
), for the
2
-
G
terms we have:

(2.66)

\tr G(z)E_{a}G(z)E_{b}=t\cdot{\cal L}_{t,(+,+),(a,b)},\quad\tr G(z)E_{a}G^{\dagger}(z)E_{b}=t\cdot{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}.

Using (
2.57
) for rank-
2

\cal K
, we derive:

t\cdot{\cal K}_{t,(+,+),(a,b)}=W^{-1}m^{2}_{sc}(z)\cdot\frac{1}{1-m^{2}_{sc}(z)\cdot S^{(B)}},\quad t\cdot{\cal K}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}=W^{-1}|m_{sc}(z)|^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1-|m_{sc}(z)|^{2}\cdot S^{(B)}}.

Therefore, (
2.6
) and (
2.7
) in Theorem
2.4
follow from (
2.60
) in the case
n=2
, while (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) follow from (
2.62
).
This completes the proof of Theorems
2.3
and
2.4
.
∎

We remark that there is a subtle difference between the
2
-loop and the
T
-observable.
By definition, the
2
-loop is given by

{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}=\sum_{i,j}(G_{t,+})_{ij}E_{a}(j)(G_{t,-})_{ji}E_{b}(i)=\sum_{i,j}|(G_{t,+})_{ij}|^{2}E_{a}(j)E_{b}(i).

Comparing
{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}
with the
T
observable (
1.1
), we find that there are two averaging over indices in the loop observable, but only one averaging in
T
. Here we neglect the unimportant difference between
S
and
E_{b}
operators.
While an extra averaging might seem to be insignificant, we remind the reader that in the special case of Wigner matrices,

N^{-1}\sum_{xy}|G_{x,y}|^{2}=(\im z)^{-1}N^{-1}\sum_{x}\im G_{xx}.

The averaging in the
x
index is critical for the local law of Wigner matrices asserting that the fluctuation of
N^{-1}\tr G
is one order smaller than that of
G_{xx}
. For similar reasons, our results for
2
-loop will not hold for the
T
observable (
1.1
).

2.7
Strategy of the proofs of main lemmas

We now outline the proofs of Lemmas
2.18
,
2.19
and
2.20
.
By Definitions
2.9
and
2.12
,

G_{0}(+)=m\cdot I_{N\times N},\quad{\cal L}_{0,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={\cal K}_{0,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}},\quad\forall\;\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a},

where
I_{N\times N}
is the identity matrix. It is easy to check that

(2.67)

Lemmas
2.18
,
2.19
and
2.20
hold at
t=0
with no error.

For
t>0
, we will prove the following theorem.

Theorem 2.21
.

Assume for some fixed
E
:
|E|\leq 2-\kappa
and
s\in[0,1]
that Lemmas
2.18
,
2.19
and
2.20
hold at time
s
, namely, for
1\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
and large
D>0
,

(2.68)

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\left|{\cal L}_{s,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}_{s,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|

\displaystyle\prec(W\ell_{s}\eta_{s})^{-n}

(2.69)

\displaystyle\left|{\cal L}_{s,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}_{s,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|

\displaystyle\prec(W\ell_{s}\eta_{s})^{-2}\exp\left(-\left|\frac{a_{1}-a_{2}}{\ell_{s}}\right|^{1/2}\right)+W^{-D},\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(+,-)

(2.70)

\displaystyle\|G^{(E)}_{s,+}-m^{(E)}\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\prec(W\ell_{s}\eta_{s})^{-1/2},

(2.71)

\displaystyle\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\left|\mathbb{E}{\cal L}_{s,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}_{s,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|

\displaystyle\prec(W\ell_{s}\eta_{s})^{-3},\quad\forall\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{+,-\}^{2}.

Then for any
t>s
satisfying

(2.72)

(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-1}\leq\left(\frac{1-t}{1-s}\right)^{30}

we have that
(
2.68
), (
2.71
), (
2.69
) and (
2.70
) hold with
s
replaced by
t
.

Proof of Lemmas
2.18
,
2.19
, and
2.20
.

For any fixed
\tau
and
t\leq 1-N^{-1+\tau}
, choose
\tau^{\prime}>0
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-1}\leq W^{-30\tau^{\prime}},\quad(1-t)=W^{-n_{0}\tau^{\prime}}

Let

1-s_{k}=W^{-k\tau^{\prime}},\quad k\leq n_{0},\quad s_{n_{0}}=t

Since
W\ell_{s}\eta_{s}
is decreasing in
s\in[0,1]
, we have

(W\ell_{s_{k+1}}\eta_{s_{k+1}})^{-1}\leq(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-1}\leq W^{-30\tau^{\prime}}\leq\left(\frac{1-s_{k}}{1-s_{k+1}}\right)^{30}

for all
k
such that
k+1\leq n_{0}
.
We can now apply Theorem
2.21
from
s_{k}
to
s_{k+1}
for
k=0
until
k=n_{0}-1
so that the conclusions of Theorem
2.21
hold for
s_{n_{0}}=t
. We have thus proved Lemmas
2.18
,
2.19
, and
2.20
. Notice that for any
\tau
fixed,
n_{0}
is a finite number depending on
\tau
. Thus we only have finite iterations. This is important because every time we apply Theorem
2.21
our inequalities deteriate by a factor

N^{\varepsilon}
. At the end, we will have a factor
N^{n_{0}\varepsilon}
at the time
t
. Since
\varepsilon
is arbitrary small, this factor is still harmless for any
\tau
fixed.
∎

Theorem
2.21
will be proved in six steps, with their detailed proofs provided in Section
5
. Throughout these steps, we assume that the conditions of Theorem
2.21
are satisfied. In addition, each step builds on the conclusions established in the preceding steps.

Step 1
(A priori loop bounds): The
n
-loop is bounded by

(2.73)

{\cal L}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\prec(\ell_{u}/\ell_{s})^{(n-1)}\cdot(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-n+1},\quad s\leq u\leq t.

Furthermore, the weak local law holds in the sense

(2.74)

\|G_{u}-m\|_{\max}\prec(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-1/4},\quad s\leq u\leq t.

Here the exponent is
1/4
instead of
1/2
in (
2.70
).

Step 2
(A priori
2
-loop decay):
The following local law holds for
u\in[s,t]
, namely,

(2.75)

\|G_{u}-m\|_{\max}\prec(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-1/2},\quad s\leq u\leq t.

Hence (
2.70
) in Theorem
2.21
holds.
In addition, with

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(+,-)
, we have for any
s\leq u\leq t
and
D>0
that

(2.76)

\left|{\cal L}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\prec\left(\eta_{s}/\eta_{u}\right)^{4}\cdot(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-2}\exp\left(-\left|\frac{a_{1}-a_{2}}{\ell_{u}}\right|^{1/2}\right)+W^{-D}.\quad

Step 3
(Sharp loop bounds):
The following sharp estimate on
n
-
G
-loop holds:

(2.77)

\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\left|{\cal L}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\prec(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-n+1},\quad\quad s\leq u\leq t,\quad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}.

Step 4
(A sharp
\cal L-\cal K
bound):
The following sharp estimate on
{\cal L}_{t}-{\cal K}_{t}
of length
n
holds:

(2.78)

\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\left|{\cal L}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\prec(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-n},\quad\quad s\leq u\leq t,\quad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}

This implies (
2.68
) in Theorem
2.21
.

Step 5
(A sharp decay bound): For
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(+,-)
,

(2.79)

\left|{\cal L}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\prec(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-2}\exp\left(-\left|\frac{a_{1}-a_{2}}{\ell_{u}}\right|^{1/2}\right)+W^{-D}.

The last bound implies (
2.69
) in Theorem
2.21
.

Step 6
(A sharp
\mathbb{E}\cal L-\cal K
bound):
The following estimate on
2
-
G
-loop with
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\{+,-\}^{2}
holds:

(2.80)

\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\left|\mathbb{E}{\cal L}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\prec(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-3},\quad s\leq u\leq t,\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\{+,-\}^{2}.

We will use Steps 1-5 to prove that (
2.68
), (
2.69
), and (
2.70
) of Theorem
2.21
hold with
s
replaced by
t
. Here (
2.71
) will not be needed for Steps 1-5, i.e., Theorem
2.21
holds if (
2.71
) was removed from both the assumption and statement.

2.8
Sum zero properties

Recall the loop hierarchy (
2.45
) of the
n
-loop is of the form

(2.81)

\displaystyle d\mathcal{L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\mathcal{E}^{(\widetilde{G})}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\mbox{ quadratic terms}.

The first two terms are linear in the loops (assuming
\tilde{G}
is given) and will be shown to be error terms. The primitive hierarchy drop these two error terms but keep the quadratic terms. The term
\mathcal{E}^{(\widetilde{G})}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
involves
n+1
-loop and the quadratic variation of
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
depends on
2n+2
loops. The quadratic term, however, involves only loops up to length
n
. Therefore, we can solve the primitive equation stating from
n=1,2\ldots
. This procedure clearly cannot be applied to the loop hierarchy.

It turns out that both
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{L}
have similar singularities as
t\to 1
in the form

\mathcal{L}\sim\left(1-t\right)^{-C_{n}}\sim{\mathcal{K}},\quad\im z\sim 1-t.

This singularity at
t\to 1
is difficult to control. It is a common phenomenon for quadratic differential equations which typically are unstable under perturbation. Since perturbations of quadratic differential equations are governed by a linear one, we consider a toy equation

\partial_{t}f=2f-c\cdot t+1,\quad f(0)=a.

This equation can be solved explicitly

f(t)=e^{2t}\big[a+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{c}{4}\big]+\frac{2ct+c-2}{4}.

If
a+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{c}{4}=0
then
f(t)=O(t)
. The subtle condition

a+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{c}{4}=0

changes the exponential growth of
f
to a linear growth! Without explicit solutions, it is not easy to prove the sub-exponential bound of the last toy equation. In our setting,
\mathcal{K}
-loops can be solved by an explicit
tree representation formula
(Lemma
3.4
) and the previous subtle condition will be implemented by a
sum-zero property
of the
\mathcal{K}
-loops. We will show that both
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{K}
-loops satisfy Ward’s identity (Lemma
3.6
). From these Ward’s identities,
we will prove a sum-zero property for the
\mathcal{K}
-loops.

2.9
Notations

Here we summarize global notations used in this paper.

•

W
is band width,
L
is the number of blocks,
N=W\times L

•

{\cal I}_{a}
is the
a
-th block,
[i]
is the block where index
i
is.
E_{a}
is the following matrix only supported on
{\cal I}_{a}
.

i\in{\cal I}_{[i]},\quad(E_{a})_{ij}=\delta_{ij}{\bf 1}(i\in{\cal I}_{a})

•

S\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
,
S^{(B)}\in\mathbb{R}^{L\times L}
,
S_{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{W\times W}
are all related to the variances of the matrix entries, and

S=S^{(B)}\otimes S_{W}

•

In the proof involving the stochastic flow, we typically omit the superscript
(E)
for simplicity. As a result, the notations
z_{t}
,
G_{t}
, and
m
are defined as follows:

z_{t}=z_{t}^{(E)},\quad G_{t}:=(\sqrt{t}H-z_{t})^{-1},\quad m=\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}m_{sc}(E+i\varepsilon).

Additionally, we use
G_{t}
to denote
(H_{t}-z_{t})^{-1}
, where
H_{t}
has the same distribution as
\sqrt{t}H
. The context will make it clear which interpretation of
G_{t}
is being applied in the proof.

•

Follow the
z_{t}
, the
\eta_{t}=\im z_{t}
and
\ell_{t}
is defined in (
2.59
) as

\ell_{t}:=\hat{\ell}(t)=\min(|1-t|^{-1/2},L)

Here
\ell_{t}\sim\ell(z_{t})\sim\ell(z)
is the decay length.

•

\cal L
is for
G
-loop,
\cal K
is for the deterministic partner of
G
-loop, and
\cal C
is for
G
-chain.

•

The propagator
\Theta_{\xi}=\Theta^{(B)}_{\xi}
is defined in Definition
2.13
. It is used to explicitly define the solution of
\mathcal{K}
in Definition
3.3
.

•

The
\Xi^{({\cal L})}
,
\Xi^{({\cal L-K})}
,
\Xi^{({\cal C})}
are ratios between these quantities and their heuristic size.

\displaystyle\Xi^{({\cal L})}_{t,m}

\displaystyle:=\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\left|{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\cdot\left(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t}\right)^{m-1}\cdot{\bf 1}(\sigma\in\{+,-\}^{m}),

\displaystyle\Xi^{({\cal L-K})}_{t,m}

\displaystyle:=\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\left|{({\cal L-K})}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\cdot\left(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t}\right)^{m}\cdot{\bf 1}(\sigma\in\{+,-\}^{m}),

\displaystyle\Xi^{({{\cal C},\;diag})}_{t,m}

\displaystyle:=\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\max_{i}\left|\left({\cal C}^{(m)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right)_{ii}\right|\cdot\left(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t}\right)^{m-1}\cdot{\bf 1}(\sigma\in\{+,-\}^{m}),

(2.82)

\displaystyle\Xi^{({{\cal C},\;off})}_{t,m}

\displaystyle:=\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\max_{i\neq j}\left|\left({\cal C}^{(m)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right)_{ij}\right|\cdot\left(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t}\right)^{m-1/2}\cdot{\bf 1}(\sigma\in\{+,-\}^{m}).

•

The operators
\varTheta_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
and
{\cal U}_{s,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
are defined in Def.
5.2
as the linear operators for the integrated loop hierarchy for
\cal L-\cal K
in Lemma
5.3
.

•

The
\cal G
operators, such as
{\cal G}^{(a)}_{k}
,
{\cal G}^{(a),\,L}_{k,l}
, and
{\cal G}^{(a),\,R}_{k,l}
, are defined in Definition
2.10
. These operators represent the cutting and gluing of Loop operators within the loop hierarchy.

•

The
\Gamma_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
represents the tree graph used in the tree representation of
\cal K
.

•

The sets
{\cal F}(\Gamma)
and
{\cal F}_{\text{long}}(\Gamma)
correspond to the non-neighboring internal edges and the neighboring long internal edges of
\Gamma
, respectively.

•

The
{\cal K}^{(\pi)}
is defined in Definition
3.9
as the sum of certain tree graphs.

•

The
\Sigma^{(\pi)}
is further defined as the self-energy of
{\cal K}^{(\pi)}
in Definition
3.9
. Certain specific
\Sigma^{(\pi)}
exhibit the sum-zero property, as demonstrated in Lemma
3.10
.

•

The
\cal E
terms represent the non-leading terms that arise in the (integrated) loop hierarchy (
2.45
) and (
5.3
). Specifically,
{\cal E}^{(M)}
and
{\cal E}^{(\widetilde{G})}
are defined in (
2.46
) and (
2.47
), respectively. The term
\mathcal{E}^{((\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\times(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K}))}
is defined in (
5.13
). Additionally,
\left(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}\right)
and
\left(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}\right)^{(k)}
are defined in Definition
5.4
.

•

The
{\cal T}^{(\cal L-\cal K)}_{t}
is defined as the tail function of
\cal L-\cal K
in (
5.26
). The
{\cal T}_{t,D}
represents the deterministic rough tail function, as defined in (
5.27
).

•

The terms
{\cal J}_{u,D}
and
\cal J^{*}
are introduced in (
5.28
) and (
5.29
), respectively, to describe the ratio between
{\cal T}^{(\cal L-\cal K)}_{t}
and
{\cal T}_{t,D}
.

•

The scale
\ell_{t}^{*}
is define as
\ell_{t}^{*}=(\log W)^{3/2}\ell_{t}
. In this scale
\Theta_{t}
is exponentially small, while
{\cal T}_{t}
is not.

•

The operators
\cal P
and
{\cal Q}_{t}
, along with the function
\vartheta
, are used to define and construct a sum-zero tensor. Their definitions can be found in Definition
5.12
.

3
Definition and properties of
\cal K

The primitive loop
{\cal K}
has an exact formula in terms of summation over tree graphs which we now present.

3.1
Tree representation of
{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}

Definition 3.1

(Canonical partition of polygon)
.

a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}
a_{5}
a_{6}
b_{1}
b_{2}
b_{3}
e_{2}
e_{3}
e_{4}
e_{5}
e_{6}
e_{1}

{\cal V}(\Gamma)=\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4},a_{5},a_{6},b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}\}

{\cal E}(\Gamma)=\{\{a_{1},b_{1}\},\{a_{2},b_{1}\},\{a_{3},b_{1}\},\{a_{4},b_{3}\},\{a_{5},b_{3}\},\{a_{6},b_{2}\},\{b_{1},b_{2}\},\{b_{2},b_{3}\},\}

{\cal F}(\Gamma)=\{\{1,4\},\{4,6\}\}

Figure 4
:
Illustration of canonical partition of the polygons

Let
{{\cal P}}_{\textbf{a}}
be an oriented polygon with vertices
\textbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},

\ldots,a_{n})
such that
a_{k}
and
a_{k+1}
are next to each other.
We will use periodic convention so that
a_{0}=a_{n}
. The edge
\overline{a_{k-1}a_{k}}
is called the
k
-th edge of
{{\cal P}}_{\textbf{a}}
. By definition,

{{\cal P}}_{(a,b,c)}\neq{{\cal P}}_{(b,c,a)}
,
since the 2nd edge of
{{\cal P}}_{(a,b,c)}
is
\overline{ab}
, while the the 2nd edge
of{{\cal P}}_{(b,c,a)}
is
\overline{bc}
.

A partition of
{{\cal P}}_{\textbf{a}}
is called canonical if and only if

•

Each sub-region in the partition is also a polygon.

•

There is one to one correspondence between the edges of the polygon and the sub-regions. Each edge
e_{k}:=\overline{a_{k-1}a_{k}}
belongs to exactly one sub-region, and each sub-region contains exactly one edge
e_{k}
. We denote the subregion containing
e_{i}
by
R_{i}
.

•

Each vertex
a_{i}
belongs to exactly two regions, i.e.,
R_{i}
and
R_{i+1}
(with
R_{1}=R_{n+1}
).

Note that following a canonical partition, the
n
-polygon (i.e., the black edges in Fig.
4
) can be compressed into a zero-area loop along the interior boundaries (i.e., the blue and purple edges in Fig.
4
).

We define the equivalent class of the canonical partition as follows: for partitions
P
and
\widetilde{P}
,

P\sim\widetilde{P}\;\iff\quad\left(\forall\;1\leq i,j\leq n,\quad\quad\quad\;R_{i}\cap R_{j}=\emptyset\iff\widetilde{R}_{i}\cap\widetilde{R}_{j}=\emptyset\right)

i.e., the sub-regions have the same neighbors.
We denote
SP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})
the collection of equivalent classes of the canonical partition of polygon
{\cal P}_{\textbf{a}}
:

SP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}}):=\{[P]:\;\hbox{$[P]$ is equivalent class of the canonical partition of polygon ${\cal P}_{\textbf{a}}$}\}

For each class of canonical partition of
n
polygon, we assign a tree structure by removing the edges of the polygon. We denote
TSP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})
the collection of trees for the classes of the canonical partitions of the polygon
{\cal P}_{\textbf{a}}
:

TSP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}}):=\left\{\Gamma:\Gamma\sim[P]\in SP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})\right\}

We divide edges of a tree
\Gamma
into two classes: 1. boundary edges consisting of any edge with a vertex in the polygon. 2. internal edges consisting of the rest. Finally, polygon edges are those edges in the original polygon.

Lemma 3.2

(Classification of canonical partitions)
.

Let
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\in TSP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})
be the tree for a canonical partition polygon
{\cal P}_{a}
.
Denote by
{\cal F}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})
as the collection of the pairs of subregions that are non-adjacent but sharing an internal edge in
\Gamma_{a}
, i.e.,

(3.1)

{\cal F}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}):=\Big\{\{i,j\}:|R_{i}\,\cap\,R_{j}|\in{\cal E}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}),\quad|i-j|>1\mod n\Big\}.

Here
\mod n
implies that
|1-n|=1
.
E.g., for the partition in Fig.
4
, we have
{\cal F}=\{\{1,4\},\{6,4\}\}
.

Then for
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\textbf{a}}
,
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\in TSP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})
, we have

\Gamma^{\prime}_{\textbf{a}}=\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\iff{\cal F}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})={\cal F}(\Gamma^{\prime}_{\textbf{a}})

Futhermore, we says
\{i,j\}
and
\{k,l\}
(with
i<j
,
k<l
) are crossing pairs if they satisfy

i<k<j<l\quad\text{or}\;k<i<l<j

where the ordering is on
\mathbb{Z}
instead of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
Then we have the following properties

1.

{\cal F}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})
contains no crossing pairs.

2.

If
{\cal F}^{*}
is a subset of
\left\{\{i,j\}:|i-j|>1,\mod\,n\right\}
and there is no crossing pairs in
{\cal F}^{*}
, then there exists a
canonical partition
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}
such that
{\cal E}^{*}={\cal F}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})
.

Proof of lemma
3.2
.

We prove this lemma by mathematical induction. First if (
3.1
) is empty, then the tree graph inside must be a star as in Figure
5
.

Figure 5:
Star graph

From now on, we assume that the set in (
3.1
) is nonempty. Assume for example that
(1,3)\in{\cal F}
. By assumption, there exists an internal edge connecting with
R_{1}
and
R_{3}
. Then the partition can be reduced to two partitions in the smaller polygons:

{\cal P}_{(a_{1},a_{2},a)},\quad\quad{\cal P}_{(a_{3},a_{4},a_{5},a_{6},b)}.

where
a
and
b
are two additional vertices to form two polygons. The rationale for this construction is that once
(1,3)\in{\cal F}
is given, the original polygon
will be divided into two regions which will not “communicate”. The vertices
a
and.
b
are added to get back to polygon language.
Based on this observation, one can easily finish the induction proof.

e_{3}
e_{4}
e_{5}
e_{6}
e_{1}
e_{2}

a_{3}

e_{4}

a
b

∎

Definition 3.3

(Representation of
{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
)
.

Assume that
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\in TSP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})
.
Associated with each edge
e_{i}
(or the corresponding region) there is charge
\sigma_{i}
and we denote by
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{+,-\}^{n}
the collection of all charges.
Let
b_{1}\cdots b_{m}
be internal vertices of the
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}
. Given
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
,

\textbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{n})
,
\textbf{b}=(b_{1}\cdots b_{m})
(here we slightly abuse the notations for the vertices and their values) and

t\in[0,1]
, define

(3.2)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}^{(\textbf{b})}\left(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}\right)=

\displaystyle\prod_{e\in{\cal E}(\Gamma)}\left(f_{t}(e)\right)_{e_{i},\,e_{f}},\quad\quad m_{i}=m(\sigma_{i})\in\{m,\overline{m}\}

here
e_{i}
and
e_{f}
are the vertices of the edge
e
, and
f(e)
is a matrix depending on the edge
e
defined as follows:

1.

If
e=\{a_{i},b_{j}\}
, then
e
is the boundary between
R_{i}
and
R_{i+1}
, and
f_{t}(e)
is defined as:

f_{t}(e)=\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}},\quad\quad e=\{a_{i},b_{j}\}

2.

If
e=\{b_{i},b_{j}\}
, and
e
is the boundary between
R_{k}
and
R_{l}
, then

f_{t}(e)=\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{k}m_{l}}-1,\quad\quad e=\{b_{i},b_{j}\},\quad R_{k}\cap R_{l}=e.

A compact definition is

(3.3)

f(e)=\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{k}m_{l}}-{\bf 1}\Big(|k-l|\neq 1,\mod n\Big),\quad\quad R_{k}\cap R_{l}=e

For
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\in TSP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})
, define

(3.4)

\Gamma_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}:=\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\sum_{\textbf{b}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{m}}\Gamma^{(\textbf{b})}_{\textbf{a}}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}).

For example
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}^{(\textbf{b})}
in Fig.
4
is given by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}^{(\textbf{b})}=

\displaystyle(\Theta^{(B)}_{t,m_{1}m_{2}})_{a_{1},b_{1}}\cdot(\Theta^{(B)}_{t,m_{2}m_{3}})_{a_{2},b_{1}}\cdot(\Theta^{(B)}_{t,m_{3}m_{4}})_{a_{3},b_{1}}\cdot(\Theta^{(B)}_{t,m_{4}m_{5}})_{a_{4},b_{3}}

\displaystyle\times(\Theta^{(B)}_{t,m_{5}m_{6}})_{a_{5},b_{3}}\cdot(\Theta^{(B)}_{t,m_{6}m_{1}})_{a_{6},b_{2}}\cdot\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t,m_{1}m_{4}}-1\right)_{b_{1},b_{2}}\cdot\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t,m_{4}m_{6}}-1\right)_{b_{2},b_{3}}

The key result in this subsection is the following representation of
{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
.

Lemma 3.4

(Tree Representation of
\cal K
)
.

For
n\geq 2
, we have

(3.5)

{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\cdot W^{-n+1}\sum_{\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\,\in\,{TSP}({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})}\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}),\quad\quad\quad m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}m(\sigma_{i});

As an example, we give the tree graph representation of
\cal K
for
n=4
.

a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}
b_{1}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}
b_{1}
b_{3}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}
