b_{2}

Figure 6:
Graphs for
n=4

There are three graphs for the case
n=4
, as in Figure
6
. The blue edges are boundary edges and equal to
\Theta^{(B)}
. The purple edges are internal edges equal to
\Theta^{(B)}-1
. The r.h.s. of (
3.5
) equals to

\displaystyle\sum_{\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\,\in\,{TSP}({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})}\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma})=

\displaystyle\sum_{b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{4}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i-1}m_{i}}\right)_{a_{i}b_{i}}\right)\times

\displaystyle\times\left(\delta_{b_{1}b_{2}b_{3}b_{4}}+\delta_{b_{1}b_{2}}\delta_{b_{3}b_{4}}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{1}m_{3}}-1\right)_{b_{1}b_{3}}+\delta_{b_{1}b_{4}}\delta_{b_{2}b_{3}}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{2}m_{4}}-1\right)_{b_{1}b_{2}}\right)

Corollary 3.5

(Pure loop
\cal K
)
.

In the special case
\sigma=(+,+\cdots+)
, we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle\left|{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right|\leq C_{n}\exp\left(-c_{n}\max_{ij}\|a_{i}-a_{j}\|\right),\quad\|a_{i}-a_{j}\|=(a_{i}-a_{j})\mod L

Proof of Corollary
3.5
.

By assumption that
\sigma_{i}=+
for all
i
,
f_{t}(e)
in (
3.3
) is either
\Theta^{(B)}_{tm^{2}}
or
\Theta^{(B)}_{tm^{2}}-1
. Applying (
2.52
) with
\xi=tm^{2}
, we obtain that
\|\Theta^{(B)}_{tm^{2}}\|_{\max}=O(1)
and
\Theta^{(B)}_{tm^{2}}
decays exponentially. Thus

\left|\Gamma^{(\textbf{b})}_{\textbf{a}}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma})\right|\leq C_{n}\exp\left(-c_{n}\max_{ij}\|b_{i}-b_{j}\|-c_{n}\max_{ij}\|a_{i}-b_{j}\|\right).

Together with the (
3.4
) and (
3.5
), we obtain the desired result (
3.6
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

In this proof, we temporally denote

\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}:=m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\cdot W^{-n+1}\sum_{\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\,\in\,{TSP}({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})}\Gamma_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}},\quad\quad\quad\quad m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}m(\sigma_{i});

We will show that

(3.7)

\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={{\cal K}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}},\quad\text{for}\;\quad t=0

and
\widetilde{\cal K}
satisfies the dynamics equation for
\cal K
in (
2.48
), i.e.,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\,\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={W}\cdot\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sum_{a,b}\;\left({\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}\circ\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right)S^{(B)}_{ab}\left({\cal G}^{(b),R}_{k,l}\circ\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right)

here

{\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}\circ\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\,=\,\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{t,\;{\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\;\textbf{a}\right)},\quad\quad\quad{\cal G}^{(b),R}_{k,l}\circ\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\,=\,\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{t,\;{\cal G}^{(b),R}_{k,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\;\textbf{a}\right)}.

For
t=0
, we have by definition that

t=0\implies\Theta^{(B)}_{t\,\xi}=I

If the tree graph
\Gamma
has an internal edge, then
\Gamma_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=0
when
t=0
. Hence for
t=0
, the only non-trivial graph is the star-shaped graph (figure.
5
), which has only one internal vertex.
Hence (
3.7
) can be explicitly verified, i.e.,

\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{0,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}W^{-n+1}\cdot\delta_{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}}=W^{-n+1}\Gamma^{(\text{star})}_{0,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={\cal K}_{0,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}.

Now we prove (
3.8
). By definition,

\frac{d}{dt}\,\widetilde{{\cal K}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}W^{-n+1}\cdot\sum_{\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\,\in\,{TSP}({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})}\frac{d}{dt}\,\Gamma_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}W^{-n+1}\cdot\sum_{\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\,\in\,{TSP}({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})}\sum_{\textbf{b}}\frac{d}{dt}\,\Gamma^{(\textbf{b})}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}

Recall
\Gamma^{(\textbf{b})}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
defined in (
3.2
).
Due to
dm_{i}/dt=0
, the derivative
d/dt
acts only on the
f_{t}(e)
. By definition,

\frac{d}{dt}\Theta^{(B)}_{t\xi}=\Theta^{(B)}_{t\xi}\cdot\xi S^{(B)}\cdot\Theta^{(B)}_{t\xi}

Therefore,

\frac{d}{dt}\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{j}}=m_{i}m_{j}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{j}}\cdot S^{(B)}\cdot\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{j}}\right)

In other words, the derivative of a blue edge
\Theta^{(B)}
or a purple edge
\Theta^{(B)}-1
equals
m_{i}m_{j}
times two blue edges with
S^{(B)}
in the middle.

\frac{d}{dt}
\frac{d}{dt}
=
S^{(B)}
\cdot\;m_{i}m_{j}

Figure 7:
Derivatives of edges

On the other hand, we know that for fixed
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\in TSP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}})
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
,
a
,
i
,
j
, there is at most one edge
e\in{\cal E}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})
such that
e=R_{i}\cap R_{j}
. Then we can write the derivative of
\Gamma_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
as follows

(3.9)

\frac{d}{dt}\,\Gamma^{(\textbf{b})}_{t,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\;\;\sum_{e\,\in\,{\cal E}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})}\textbf{1}\left(e=R_{i}\cap R_{j}\right)\cdot\Gamma^{(\textbf{b})}_{t,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}}\cdot\frac{m_{i}\,m_{j}\,\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{j}}\cdot S^{(B)}\cdot\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{j}}\right)_{e_{i},e_{f}}}{\left(f(e)\right)_{e_{i},e_{f}}}

where
f_{t}(e)
is defined in (
3.3
).
Suppose that there exists
e\,\in\,{\cal E}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})
such that
e=R_{i}\cap R_{j}
. Then

\Gamma^{(\textbf{b})}_{t,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}}\cdot\frac{\,\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{j}}\cdot S^{(B)}\cdot\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{j}}\right)_{e_{i},e_{f}}}{\left(f(e)\right)_{e_{i},e_{f}}},\quad e=R_{i}\cap R_{j}

is equal to removing the edge
e
in
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}
and adding two edges
\{e_{i},a\}
,
\{e_{f},b\}
, and a
S^{(B)}_{ab}
in the middle. Here is an example with
i=1
and
j=3
in Figure
8
. Note: this statement also holds for the case
|i-j|=1
. For example, if
i=1
,
j=2
, the triangle in the r.h.s. of Figure
8
will become a
n=2
polygon
{\cal P}_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}),(a_{1},a)}
with a blue edge
(a_{1},a)
inside.

Derivative of tree

The new edges created by the partition will inherit the original charges. We denote them by

(3.10)

(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime})={\cal G}^{(a),L}_{i,j}(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})\quad\quad(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime\prime})={\cal G}^{(b),R}_{i,j}(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}).

Therefore, there exist

\Gamma^{\prime}_{\textbf{a}^{\prime}}\in TSP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}^{\prime}})
and
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{\textbf{a}^{\prime\prime}}\in TSP({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}^{\prime\prime}})
such that (as in Figure
8
)

m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\cdot\sum_{\textbf{b}}\Gamma^{(\textbf{b})}_{t,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}}\cdot\frac{m_{i}\,m_{j}\,\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{j}}\cdot S^{(B)}\cdot\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{j}}\right)_{e_{i},e_{f}}}{\left(f(e)\right)_{e_{i},e_{f}}}=\sum_{a,b}m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime}}\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}}\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime\prime}}.

Thus,

m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\cdot\frac{d}{dt}\sum_{\Gamma}\Gamma_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\sum_{i<j}\sum_{a,b}\left(m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}}\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\Gamma^{\prime}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime}}\right)\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot\left(m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}}\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}\Gamma^{\prime\prime}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime\prime}}\right)

where
i
,
j
,
a
,
b
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
,
a
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}
,
\textbf{a}^{\prime}
,
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime\prime}
, and
\textbf{a}^{\prime\prime}
satisfy relation (
3.10
). Clearly it is equivalent to (
3.8
) and this completes the proof of Lemma
3.4
.

∎

3.2
Ward’s identity on
\cal K

Recall Ward’s identity on resolvents stating that

G\cdot G^{\dagger}=G(z)\cdot G^{\dagger}(z)=G(z)\cdot G(\overline{z})=\frac{G(z)-G(\overline{z})}{z-\overline{z}}=\frac{G-G^{\dagger}}{2\eta},\quad\eta=\im z.

In our setting,
G_{t}(+)=(H_{t}-z_{t})^{-1}=\left(G_{t}(-)\right)^{\dagger}
and thus

(3.11)

G_{t}(+)\cdot G_{t}(-)=\frac{G_{t}(+)-G_{t}(-)}{2\eta_{t}}=\frac{G_{t}(+)-G_{t}(-)}{2(1-t)\im m},\quad\eta_{t}=\im z_{t}.

From Ward’s identity, we have the following identity for
2-G
loop:

\sum_{a}{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}=\frac{1}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal L}_{t,(+),(b)}-{\cal L}_{t,(-),(b)}\right).

We extend this identity to all
n-G
loops
{\cal L}
in the following lemma. The main purpose of this subsection is show that the same identity holds for
\cal K

loop as well.

Lemma 3.6
.

For
n-G
loop
{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
with

\sigma_{1}=+
and
\sigma_{n}=-
,
we have

(3.12)

\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\frac{1}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal L}_{t,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}+,\;\textbf{a}/a_{n}}-{\cal L}_{t,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}-,\;\textbf{a}/a_{n}}\right)

and

(3.13)

\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\frac{1}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal K}_{t,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}+,\;\textbf{a}/a_{n}}-{\cal K}_{t,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}-,\;\textbf{a}/a_{n}}\right)

where

•

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\pm
is obtained by removing
\sigma_{n}
from
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
and replacing
\sigma_{1}
with
\pm
, i.e.,

\boldsymbol{\sigma}\pm=(\pm,\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\cdots\sigma_{n-1}).

Notice that the length of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\pm
is
n-1
.

•

\textbf{a}/a_{n}
is obtained by removing
a_{n}
from
a
:

\textbf{a}/a_{n}=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},\cdots,a_{n-1})

Corollary 3.7
.

Under the assumption of Lemma
3.6
, we have

(3.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{2}}\cdots\sum_{a_{n-1}}\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=O(W\eta_{t})^{-n+1}

Proof of Corollary
3.7
.

By definition of
\cal K
, we know that
{\cal K}
is translation invariant. Then the left side of equation (
3.14
) is independent of
a_{1}
.
Hence it is equivalent to

\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\textbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{n}}{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=O(W\eta_{t})^{-n+1}.

Applying (
3.13
) repeatedly, we can reduce it to shorter pure loops to get

(3.15)

\displaystyle\Big|L^{-1}\cdot\sum_{\textbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{n}}{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Big|\leq C_{n}\sum_{1\leq m\leq n}(W\eta_{t})^{-n+m}\cdot L^{-1}\cdot\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\{+\}^{m}\cup\{-\}^{m}}\left|\sum_{\textbf{a}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{m}}{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime}}\right|,\quad

Here
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}\in\{+\}^{m}\cup\{-\}^{m}
means that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}=(+,+,\cdots,+)\;\hbox{or }(-,-,\cdots,-)
.
By the estimate of pure loop
\cal K
in Lemma
3.5
, we have

\sum_{\textbf{a}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{m}}{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime}}=O(1).

Together with (
3.15
), this completes the proof of the corollary.

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.

The equation (
3.12
) for
\cal L
follows from (
3.11
) directly. For the (
3.13
), we consider first that
n=2
.
By the explicit formula for
\cal K
in this case, we have

(3.16)

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{2}}{\cal K}_{t,(+,-),(a_{1},a_{2})}=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{a_{2}}\left(\frac{|m^{2}|}{1-t|m|^{2}S^{(B)}}\right)_{a_{1}a_{2}}=\frac{1}{W(1-t)}

On the other hand, by definition, we have

\frac{1}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal K}_{t,(+),(a_{1})}-{\cal K}_{t,(-),(a_{1})}\right)=\frac{m-\overline{m}}{2W\eta_{t}}=\frac{1}{W(1-t)},\quad m:=m^{(E)}

Here we used
|m|=1
and
\eta_{t}=(1-t)\im m
. Combining these two identities, we have proved (
3.13
) for
n=2
.
Similarly, the case
n=3
follows from a direct calculation and the following identities

\left(\frac{|m^{2}|}{1-t|m|^{2}S^{(B)}}\right)-\left(\frac{m^{2}}{1-tm^{2}S^{(B)}}\right)=\left(1-m^{2}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{1-t|m|^{2}S^{(B)}}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{1-tm^{2}S^{(B)}}\right).

and

1-m^{2}=m(\overline{m}-m)

For
n\geq 4
, we will use the primitive equation instead of the tree representation. For simplicity, we temporally denote

{\cal K}^{(*)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\sum_{a_{1}}{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}},\quad{\cal K}^{(**)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\frac{1}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal K}^{(+)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}^{(-)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right),

(3.17)

{\cal K}^{(+)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}:={\cal K}_{t,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}+,\;\textbf{a}/a_{n}}\quad\quad{\cal K}^{(-)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}:={\cal K}_{t,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}-,\;\textbf{a}/a_{n}}

Our goal is to prove
{\cal K}^{(*)}={\cal K}^{(**)}
. With

m_{n}=\overline{m},\quad m_{1}=m,\quad|m|=1,\quad\eta_{t}\big|_{t=0}=\im m.

By Definition
2.12
,

\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal K}_{0,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=W^{-n+1}\cdot\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\right){\bf 1}(a_{1}=a_{3}=\cdots=a_{n-1})=\frac{1}{2W\eta_{0}}\left({\cal K}^{(+)}_{0,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma},\;\textbf{a}}-{\cal K}^{(-)}_{0,\;\boldsymbol{\sigma},\;\textbf{a}}\right).

This implies that

{\cal K}^{(*)}_{0,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={\cal K}^{(**)}_{0,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}.

In the remainder of this subsection, under the inductive assumption that
{\cal K}^{(*)}={\cal K}^{(**)}
holds for
{\cal K}^{(*)}
of lengths strictly less than
n
, we will prove the following identity:

(3.18)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left({\cal K}^{(*)}-{\cal K}^{(**)}\right)_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}

\displaystyle\;=\;\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\sum_{a\;b}\left[\left({\cal K}^{(*)}-{\cal K}^{(**)}\right)_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{k}\to a)\right]\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot{\cal K}_{t,(\sigma_{k},\sigma_{k+1}),(a_{k},b)}

\displaystyle\;+\;\frac{1}{1-t}\cdot\left({\cal K}^{(*)}-{\cal K}^{(**)}\right)_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}

where
(a_{k}\to a)
means replacing
a_{k}
in
a
by
a
. Together with
{\cal K}^{(*)}-{\cal K}^{(**)}=0
at
t=0
, this linear differential equation has only the trivial solution, i.e.,

{\cal K}^{(*)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={\cal K}^{(**)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}},\quad 0\leq t\leq 1.

For the l.h.s. of (
3.18
), by the primitive equation for
\cal K
, we have

(3.19)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\cal K}^{(*)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\sum_{a_{n}}\frac{d}{dt}\,{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}={W}\sum_{a_{n}}\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sum_{a\;b}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab},

where

{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}:=\left({\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}\circ{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right),\quad{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}:=\left({\cal G}^{(b),R}_{k,l}\circ{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right).

By definition, the index
a_{n}
appears in above
{\cal J}^{L}
. On the other hand,
\frac{d}{dt}\eta_{t}^{-1}=(1-t)^{-1}\eta_{t}^{-1}
and thus

(3.20)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}{\cal K}^{(**)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\frac{1}{1-t}\cdot{\cal K}^{(**)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\frac{W}{2W\eta_{t}}\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n-1}\sum_{a\;b}\left({\cal J}^{L,(+)}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{k,l,b}-{\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R,(-)}_{k,l,b}\right)\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab},\quad

where (recall
\cal K^{(\pm)}
defined in (
3.17
))

{\cal J}^{L,(\pm)}_{k,l,a}:=\left({\cal G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}\circ{\cal K}^{(\pm)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right),\quad{\cal J}^{R,(\pm)}_{k,l,b}:=\left({\cal G}^{(b),R}_{k,l}\circ{\cal K}^{(\pm)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right).

We now demonstrate that the right-hand sides of (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) are identical, up to terms involving
({\cal K}^{*}-{\cal K}^{**})
. Specifically, the special case where
k=1
and
l=n
in (
3.19
) contributes the term
\frac{1}{1-t}\cdot{\cal K}^{(*)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
. In most other cases, we find that

{\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{k,l,b}={\cal J}^{R,(-)}_{k,l,b}={\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b},

while

{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}

can be expressed using

{\cal J}^{L,(\pm)}_{k,l,a}

by inductive assumption. For the remaining few cases (e.g.,
k=1
,
l=2
), direct cancellations occur between the right-hand sides of (
3.19
) and (
3.20
). The following proof provides a detailed, albeit tedious, argument.

The following identities can be easily verified from their definitions. These identities rely on the fact that
{\cal K}^{(+)}
and
{\cal K}^{(-)}
differ only in the first component of
\sigma
being
+
or
-
. Similarly,
{\cal K}^{(\pm)}
and
{\cal K}
differ slightly in their definitions.

•

For
k,l\in[[2,n-1]]
,

(3.21)

{\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{k,l,b}={\cal J}^{R,(-)}_{k,l,b}={\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b},\quad k,l\in[[2,n-1]]

•

For
k,l\in[[2,n-1]]
and
l-k\geq 2
, the length of
{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}
is no longer than
n-1
. By induction, we have

(3.22)

\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}-\frac{1}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{L,(+)}_{k,l,a}-{\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{k,l,a}\right)=0,\quad k,l\in[[2,n-1]],\;l-k\geq 2

•

For
k,l\in[[2,n-1]]
and
l=k+1
,

{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}={\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Big|(a_{k}\to a),\quad{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}={\cal K}_{t,(\sigma_{k},\sigma_{k+1}),(a_{k},b)},\quad k,l\in[[2,n-1]],\quad l=k+1

Similarly,

{\cal J}^{L,(\pm)}_{k,l,a}={\cal K}^{(\pm)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Big|(a_{k}\to a).

Therefore, under the same conditions on
k,l
,

(3.23)

\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}-\frac{1}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{L,(+)}_{k,l,a}-{\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{k,l,a}\right)=\left({\cal K}^{(*)}-{\cal K}^{(**)}\right)_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{k}\to a).

Combining the identities (
3.23
), (
3.21
) and (
3.22
), we can bound the following parts in (
3.20
) and (
3.19
) with
\cal K^{(*)}-\cal K^{(**)}

(3.24)

\displaystyle\sum_{2\leq k<l\leq n-1}\sum_{a\;b}\left({W}\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}-\frac{W}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{L,(+)}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{k,l,b}-{\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R,(-)}_{k,l,b}\right)\right)\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{n-2}\sum_{a\;b}\left[\left({\cal K}^{(*)}-{\cal K}^{(**)}\right)_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{k}\to a)\right]\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot{\cal K}_{t,(\sigma_{k},\sigma_{k+1}),(a_{k},b)}

Next, we estimate the cases that
k=1
or
l=n
.

•

For
k=1
and
l=n
, we have

{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}={\cal K}_{t,\,(+,\,-),\,(a,\,a_{n})},\quad{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}={\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{n}\to b)

and thus

(3.25)

\displaystyle{W}\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}\cdot S_{ab}^{(B)}=\frac{1}{1-t}\cdot{\cal K}^{(*)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}.

•

(3.26)

k=1,\;l=m,\quad\hbox{and}\quad k=m,\;l=n

for some
3\leq m\leq n-2
. By induction,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}-\frac{1}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{L,(+)}_{1,\,m,\,a}-{\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{1,\,m,\,a}\right)=0,\quad k=1,\;l=m

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}-\frac{1}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{1,\,m,\,a}-{\cal J}^{R,(-)}_{1,\,m,\,a}\right)=0,\quad k=m,\;l=n

and

(3.28)

\displaystyle{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}={\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{1,m,b},\quad k=1,\;l=m

\displaystyle{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}={\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{1,m,b},\quad k=m,\;l=n

Hence for calculating the
\sum_{ab}\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}S_{ab}
, one will see the following terms for
(k,l)=(1,m)
and
(k,l)=(m,n)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2W\eta_{t}}\sum_{ab}{\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{1,\,m,\,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{1,\,m,\,b}S_{ab}\quad

\displaystyle for\quad(k,l)=(1,m)

\displaystyle\quad\frac{-1}{2W\eta_{t}}\sum_{ab}{\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{1,\,m,\,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R,(-)}_{1,\,m,\,b}S_{ab}\quad

\displaystyle for\quad(k,l)=(m,n)

They cancel each other, therefore

(3.29)

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}^{*}\sum_{a\;b}\left({W}\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}-\frac{W}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{L,(+)}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{k,l,b}-{\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R,(-)}_{k,l,b}\right)\right)\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}=0;

here
\sum_{kl}^{*}
denote summing over
k,l
satisfying (
3.26
).

•

Since we assume that
n\geq 4
, there are only four cases left, i.e.,

(3.30)

(k,l)=(1,2),\;(1,n-1),\;(2,n),\;(n-1,n).

By definition,

(3.31)

\displaystyle(k,l)=(1,2)\quad

\displaystyle\quad{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}={\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{1}\to a),

\displaystyle{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}={\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{1,\,2,\,b}\quad

\displaystyle(k,l)=(1,n-1)\quad

\displaystyle\quad{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}={\cal K}_{t,\,(+,\;\sigma_{n-1},\,-),\,(a,\,a_{n-1},\,a_{n})},

\displaystyle{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}={\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{1,\,{n-1},\,b}

\displaystyle(k,l)=(2,n)\quad

\displaystyle\quad{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}={\cal K}_{t,\,(+,\;\sigma_{2},\,-),\,(a_{1},\,a,\,a_{n})},

\displaystyle{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}={\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{1,\,2,\,b}\quad

\displaystyle(k,l)=(n-1,n)\quad

\displaystyle\quad{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}={\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{n-1}\to a),

\displaystyle{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}={\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{1,\,{n-1},\,b}

Summing up
a_{n}
and multiplying
W
, we obtain that

(3.32)

\displaystyle(k,l)=(1,2)\quad

\displaystyle\quad W\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}={\cal K}^{(*)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{1}\to a),

\displaystyle(k,l)=(1,n-1)\quad

\displaystyle\quad W\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}=\frac{W}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal K}_{t,\,(+,\;\sigma_{n-1}),\,(a,\,a_{n-1})}-{\cal K}_{t,\,(\sigma_{n-1},-),\,(a_{n-1},\,a)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\;=\frac{W}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{L,(+)}_{1,n-1,a}-{\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{1,n-1,a}\right)

\displaystyle(k,l)=(2,n)\quad

\displaystyle\quad W\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}=\frac{W}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal K}_{t,\,(+,\;\sigma_{2}),\,(a,\,a_{2})}-{\cal K}_{t,\,(\sigma_{2},-),\,(a_{2},\,a)}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\;=\frac{W}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{1,\,2,\,a}-{\cal J}^{R,(-)}_{1,\,2,\,a}\right)

\displaystyle(k,l)=(n-1,n)\quad

\displaystyle\quad W\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}={\cal K}^{(*)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{n-1}\to a),

On the other hand, we have

(3.33)

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{(**)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{1}\to a)

\displaystyle=\frac{W}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{L,(+)}_{1,\,2,\,a}-{\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{1,\,2,\,a}\right)

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{(**)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{n-1}\to a)

\displaystyle=\frac{W}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{1,\,n-1,\,a}-{\cal J}^{R,(-)}_{1,\,n-1,\,a}\right)

Therefore, with
\sum_{kl}^{**}
denoting summing
k,l
in (
3.30
), we have

(3.34)

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}^{**}\sum_{a\;b}\left({W}\sum_{a_{n}}{\cal J}^{L}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R}_{k,l,b}-\frac{W}{2W\eta_{t}}\left({\cal J}^{L,(+)}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R,(+)}_{k,l,b}-{\cal J}^{L,(-)}_{k,l,a}\cdot{\cal J}^{R,(-)}_{k,l,b}\right)\right)\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\left({\bf 1}_{k=1}+{\bf 1}_{k=n-1}\right)\sum_{a\;b}\left[\left({\cal K}^{(*)}-{\cal K}^{(**)}\right)_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Bigg|(a_{k}\to a)\right]\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot{\cal K}_{t,(\sigma_{k},\sigma_{k+1})_{a_{k},b}}

At last, combining the identities (
3.34
), (
3.29
), (
3.25
), (
3.24
), (
3.19
) and (
3.20
), we obtain the desired result (
3.18
) and prove the Lemma
3.6
by induction.
∎

3.3
Sum zero property of
\cal K

Recall the tree representation of
\cal K
in Lemma
3.4
.
The key quantity
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma})
in this representation contains only three types of edges, namely,

S^{(B)},\quad\Theta^{(B)}_{tm^{2}},\quad\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}}

which commute one another. By explicit computations, we have

(3.35)

\displaystyle\left\|\Theta^{(B)}_{t\,m^{2}}\right\|_{\max}\prec 1,\quad\left\|\Theta^{(B)}_{t\,|m|^{2}}\right\|_{\max}\prec\frac{1}{\ell_{t}\eta_{t}};

(3.36)

\displaystyle\left\|\Theta^{(B)}_{tm^{2}}\right\|_{1}\prec 1,\quad\left\|\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}}\right\|_{1}\prec\frac{1}{\eta_{t}}

Due to the big difference in ranges between
\Theta^{(B)}_{tm^{2}}
and
\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}}
, we call them short and long edges respectively:

\Theta^{(B)}_{\,t\xi}\;\hbox{or}\;\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{\,t\xi}-1\right)=\begin{cases}\;\hbox{short $\Theta$ edge},\quad&\xi=m^{2},\;\overline{m}^{2}\\
\;\hbox{long $\Theta$ edge},\quad&\xi=|m|^{2}\end{cases}

Definition 3.8
.

Fix two sequences
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\cdots,\sigma_{n})
and
\textbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{n})
. For a partition
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\in TSP(\mathcal{P}_{\textbf{a}})
, recall that
{\cal F}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})
(
3.1
) is the collection of pairs of sub-regions that are non-adjacent but share an internal edge in
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}
. There is a one-to-one correspondence between the elements of
{\cal F}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})
and the internal edges in
{\cal E}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})
.

Given
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
, we define
{\cal F}_{\text{long}}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
to be the subset of
{\cal F}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})
corresponding to long internal edges, i.e.,

{\cal F}_{\text{long}}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}},\boldsymbol{\sigma}):=\Big\{\{i,j\}\in{\cal F}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}):\{\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}\}=\{+,-\}\Big\}.

In other words, for
\{i,j\}\in{\cal F}_{\text{long}}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}},\boldsymbol{\sigma})
, there exists an internal (i.e., purple) edge in
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}
separating
R_{i}
and
R_{j}
with
\sigma_{i}\neq\sigma_{j}
.

By definition,

{\cal F}_{\text{long}}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}},\boldsymbol{\sigma})\subset{\cal F}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}})\subset\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{\text{off}}:=\big\{\{i,j\}:1\leq i<j\leq n,\quad|i-j|\neq 1\mod n\big\}.

For a subset
\pi
satisfying
\pi\subset\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{\text{off}}
, we denote by

TSP(\mathcal{P}_{\textbf{a}},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\pi):=\big\{\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\in TSP(\mathcal{P}_{\textbf{a}}):{\cal F}_{\text{long}}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}},\boldsymbol{\sigma})=\pi\big\},\quad\pi\subset\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{\text{off}},

the subset of
TSP(\mathcal{P}_{\textbf{a}})
with
\pi
as the collection of long internal edges (which may be empty, i.e.,
\pi=\emptyset
).

Example
: In the case
\pi=\emptyset
,
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}
contains no internal long edges. We will ignore all short edges
and use a big dot representing some tree structure of consisting entirely of short edges.
In previous band papers
[
49
]
and
[
50
]
, we called this dot a molecule.

Then
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}
has the following structure. The big dots are connected via internal long edges. Locally, each sub-tree containing a red dot
{\cal M}
and the edges connecting with this
\cal M
matches a tree structure of a single-molecule partition. More precisely, for these 4 local sub-trees, we have

(3.38)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(top)}\in\;

\displaystyle\;TSP({\cal P}_{(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4},c_{1})},(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{4},\sigma_{5}),\pi=\emptyset)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(left)}\in\;

and

\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}
. We only use them to represent the local structure.

Definition 3.9

(Definition of
\cal K^{(\pi)}
and
\Sigma^{(\pi)}
)
.

Given a subset

(3.39)

\pi\subset\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{off}

define

(3.40)

{\cal K}^{(\pi)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\sum_{\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}\,\in\,{TSP}({\cal P}_{\textbf{a}},\;\boldsymbol{\sigma},\;\pi)}\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}).

where
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma})
was defined in Definition
3.3
.
Clearly,

(3.41)

{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=W^{-n+1}\cdot m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\cdot\sum_{\pi}{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}^{(\pi)},\quad m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\prod_{i}m_{i}.

Notice that there is a factor
m_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}W^{-n+1}
in the last equation due to our convention that
\cal K^{(\pi)}
is independent of
W
.
Next, we define the self energy
\Sigma^{(\pi)}
of
{\cal K}^{(\pi)}
. To this end, we relabel vertices by introducing

d_{i}
as the ending vertex of the boundary edge starting from
a_{i}
. When two edges ending at
d_{i},d_{j}
join, we identify them by adding a delta function. We then label all other internal vertices by
s_{1}
,
s_{2},\ldots
.
Define the self energy by removing from
{\cal K}^{(\pi)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
the boundary edges and then summing all
s
indices.
Clearly, we have

(3.42)

\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d}):\quad{\cal K}^{(\pi)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\sum_{\textbf{d}}\left(\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})\right)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}

Example
: For Figure
6
with
n=4
and

\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(+,-,+,-)
,

\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})=\delta_{d_{1}d_{2}d_{3}d_{4}}+\delta_{d_{1}d_{2}}\delta_{d_{3}d_{4}}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm^{2}}-1\right)_{d_{1}d_{3}}+\delta_{d_{1}d_{4}}\delta_{d_{2}d_{3}}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t\overline{m}^{2}}-1\right)_{d_{1}d_{2}}.

On the other hand, if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(+,-,+,-)
then

\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})=0
if
\pi\neq\emptyset
.

By (
2.52
) and explicit calculations, we have

\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})=O(1)

and that
\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})
is short-ranged in the sense that

(3.43)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})\leq C_{n}\exp\left(-c_{n}\max_{ij}\|d_{i}-d_{j}\|\right).

In addition, we have the following sum zero property :

\sum_{d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}}\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})=O(1-t)=O(\eta_{t}).

(The name sum zero comes from the fact that the above quantity equals to
0
when
t=1
)
Due to the translation invariance, the last bound is equivalent to

L^{-1}\cdot\sum_{d_{1},d_{2},d_{3},d_{4}}\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})=O(1-t)=O(\eta_{t}).

It turns out that this property holds for all alternating
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
with
\pi=\emptyset
.

Lemma 3.10

(Sum zero)
.

For fixed
4\leq n\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and an alternating loop
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(alt)}
with

1\leq k\leq n,\quad\quad{\sigma}^{(alt)}_{k}=\begin{cases}+,\quad k\in 2\mathbb{Z}-1\\
-,\quad k\in 2\mathbb{Z}\end{cases}\;\;,\quad t\in[0,1],

the single molecule tree graphs (i.e.,
\pi=\emptyset
) have the following sum zero property

(3.44)

L^{-1}\cdot\sum_{\textbf{d}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(alt)},\textbf{d})=O(1-t)=O(\eta_{t})

This sum-zero property is the key input for the following estimates on
\cal K^{(\pi)}
and
\cal K
.

Lemma 3.11

(Bound on
{\cal K}
)
.

For any
{\cal K}^{(\pi)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
defined in Definition
3.9
, we have

(3.45)

{\cal K}^{(\pi)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=O_{\prec}\left(\ell_{t}\cdot\eta_{t}\right)^{-n+1}

Together with (
3.41
),
we have

(3.46)

{\cal K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=O_{\prec}\left(W\ell_{t}\cdot\eta_{t}\right)^{-n+1}.

Notice that there is no
W
factor in
{\cal K}^{(\pi)}
due to its definition.

3.4
Proof of Lemma
3.10

Proof of Lemma
3.10
.

By definitions of
{\cal K}^{(\pi)}
and
\Sigma^{(\pi)}
, for any
n\geq 3
we have that

(3.47)

\sum_{\textbf{a}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=\sum_{\textbf{a}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}\cdot\sum_{\textbf{d}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})

Use
S^{(B)}\bf 1=\bf 1
, we have

\sum_{a_{i}}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}=\frac{1}{1-tm_{i}m_{i+1}}.

Therefore, we have

(3.48)

\sum_{\textbf{a}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})\Bigg/\sum_{\textbf{d}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})\sim\eta_{t}^{-\big|\{\;i:\;\sigma_{i}\neq\sigma_{i+1},\;i\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}\}\big|}.

By(
3.41
) and Corollary
3.7
, we have

(3.49)

\displaystyle L^{-1}\cdot\sum_{\pi}\sum_{\textbf{a}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=O(\eta_{t})^{-n+1}.

We now show that it holds without sum over
\pi
, i.e., for any fixed
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
,
n\geq 3
and
\pi
in (
3.39
),

(3.50)

\displaystyle L^{-1}\cdot\sum_{\textbf{a}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=O(\eta_{t})^{-n+1}

Assuming that the last equation holds in the case that

\pi=\emptyset
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(alt)}
,
together with (
3.48
), we have

(3.51)

L^{-1}\cdot\sum_{\textbf{d}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(alt)},\textbf{d})=O(\eta_{t}).

This implies the desired result (
3.44
).

We now prove (
3.50
) by induction. If
n=3
, then
\pi
can only be
\emptyset
. Hence (
3.49
) implies (
3.50
) in the case
n=3
. Next, we assume that (
3.50
) holds with
n
replaced by
m<n
. Under this assumption, we first prove that if
\pi\neq\emptyset
then (
3.50
) holds, i.e.,

(3.52)

\displaystyle\pi\neq\emptyset\implies L^{-1}\cdot\sum_{\textbf{a}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=O(\eta_{t})^{-n+1}

If
\pi\neq\emptyset
, we can always represent

{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})
with the molecule structure and the self-energy
\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}
. For example, for
n=10
and
\pi
in (
3.37
), the molecule structure is the one in Figure
10
. Then as shown in the Figure
11
, where
d_{i}
is the vertex connecting with
a_{i}
’s (which ere not marked in the figure) and
c_{i}
are the vertices of edges connecting molecules (i.e., big dots) :

{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=\sum_{\textbf{d}}\sum_{\textbf{c}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{10}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{3}\Big(\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}-1\Big)_{c_{2k-1}c_{2k}}\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{4}{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{d}^{(k)})

Here
{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{d}^{(k)})
,
1\leq k\leq 4
,
represent the four self-energies (i.e., four big dots, top, bottom, left, right). More precisely,

\textbf{d}^{(top)}=(c_{1},d_{1},d_{2},d_{3},d_{4}),\quad\textbf{d}^{(left)}=(c_{6},d_{8},d_{9},d_{10}),\quad\textbf{d}^{(bottom)}=(c_{2},c_{4},d_{7},c_{5}),\quad\textbf{d}^{(right)}=(c_{3},d_{5},d_{6}),\quad

and
\cal K^{(\pi)}
is decomposed into three parts:

•

The edges connect with external vertices, i.e., (blue) boundary edges.

•

The edges connect two different molecules, which is always
\left(\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}-1\right)

•

The cores
{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{d}^{(k)})
for each single molecule.

One can easily extend it to the general cases. Given a set

\pi
(which can be the empty set), we label all vertices of long internal edges for
\pi
by
c_{i}
,
(1\leq i\leq 2|\pi|)
. Recall that the vertex connecting with the
boundary vertex
a_{i}
is denoted by
d_{i}
. We now denote all internal vertices other than
d_{i},c_{j}
by
s_{k}
.
The indices
k
is a finite set less than
n
, but we will not specify it.
We now explain how to construct the molecule and their tree structure. Given
\{i,j\}\in\pi
, we draw a line in the polygon from the center of edge
i
to
that of
j
. In this way, we have a partition of the polygon. The set
\pi
for which there is
\Gamma_{\textbf{a}}
such that
{\cal F}_{long}(\Gamma_{\textbf{a}},\sigma)=\pi
satisfies that these lines representing the pairing are non-crossing. From now on, we will call
\pi
non-crossing pairing.
Given a non-crossing pairing, we divide the polygon into several regions, say,
M
regions (note
M=|\pi|+1
. We represent each region by a big dot (molecule), and there is an edge connecting two dots if and only if these two regions are neighboring. Notice that each dot typically has many vertices connecting to it, as shown in Figure
12
.

a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}
a_{5}
a_{6}
a_{7}
a_{8}
a_{9}
a_{10}
a_{1}
a_{2}
a_{3}
a_{4}

a_{6}
a_{7}
a_{8}
a_{9}
a_{10}

Figure 12:
\pi=\left\{\{1,5\},\{5,7\},\{8,1\}\right\}

In general, there are complicated structures inside these molecules; there are short edges and other vertices labeled by
s_{k}
. All vertices labeled by
s_{k}

are required to be summed.
With this convention, for
\pi
with
M
molecules, we can write

(3.53)

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=\sum_{\textbf{d}}\sum_{\textbf{c}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{M-1}\Big(\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}-1\Big)_{c_{2k-1}c_{2k}}\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{M}{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{d}^{(k)})

Here the vertices labelled by
s_{k}
are summed and thus they no longer appear explicitly in the formula above.
Summing over
a
, we have

(3.54)

\displaystyle\sum_{\textbf{a}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=\sum_{\textbf{a},\,\textbf{d},\,\textbf{c}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{M-1}\Big(\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}-1\Big)_{c_{2k-1}c_{2k}}\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{M}{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{d}^{(k)})

Given a molecule structure (or equivalently a set
\pi
representing non-crossing pairings), there must be a molecule containing just one
c
vertex, i.e., the big dot for this molecule connects to only one
(\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m^{2}|-1})_{c_{2k-1},c_{2k}}
edge. (For example, the top, left and right molecules in Figure
11
).
In a different language, this molecule represents a region with exactly one paring line.
For simplicity, we assume that it is the first molecule containing
c_{1}
and connecting with
a_{1},a_{2}\cdots a_{m-1}
.
With these notations, we have

(3.55)

\{1,m\}\in\pi,\quad\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{m}

The expression in the formula of
{\cal K}^{(\pi)}
related to this molecule is

\sum_{d_{1},\cdots,d_{m-1}}\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}\Bigg(t,(\sigma_{1},\cdots\sigma_{m}),(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m-1},c_{1})\Bigg)\cdot\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)

(For example: the top right part in Figure
11
is for the case
m=5
.) Notice that
d_{i},a_{i},1\leq i\leq m-1
do not appear in other molecules. The following part is separated from other parts in (
3.54
), i.e.,

f^{*}(c_{1}):=\sum_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m-1}}\sum_{d_{1},\cdots,d_{m-1}}\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}\Bigg(t,(\sigma_{1},\cdots\sigma_{m}),(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m-1},c_{1})\Bigg)\cdot\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)

Now we can write the
\cal K^{(\pi)}
in (
3.54
) in the terms of
f^{*}(c_{1})
as follows:

(3.56)

\displaystyle\sum_{\textbf{a}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=

\displaystyle\sum_{c_{1}}f^{*}(c_{1})\cdot\sum_{a_{m},\cdots,a_{n}}\sum_{d_{m},\cdots,d_{n}}\sum_{c_{2},\cdots,c_{2M-2}}

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{i=m}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{M-1}\Big(\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}-1\Big)_{c_{2k-1}c_{2k}}\cdot\prod_{k=2}^{M}{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{d}^{(k)})

In the Figure
11
, the upper right part represents the
f^{*}(c_{1})
and the lower right part presents the 2nd line of the (
3.56
).

Due to the translation invariant,
f^{*}(c_{1})
does not depend on
c_{1}
, i.e.,

f^{*}(c_{1})=f^{*}(1)\in\mathbb{C}
.
Inserting it back to (
3.56
), we obtain that

(3.57)

\displaystyle\sum_{\textbf{a}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=f^{*}(1)

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{a_{m},\cdots,a_{n}}\;\sum_{d_{m},\cdots,d_{n}}\;\sum_{\textbf{c}}

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{i=m}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{M-1}\Big(\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}-1\Big)_{c_{2k-1}c_{2k}}\cdot\prod_{k=2}^{M}{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{d}^{(k)})

Since
c_{1}
appears only in the internal edge
(\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}-1)_{c_{1}c_{2}}
, we can sum over
c_{1}
. By definition of

\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}
,
S^{(B)}\bf 1=\bf 1
and
|m|=1
, we have

\sum_{a}\left(\Theta_{t|m|^{2}}-1\right)_{ab}=\frac{t}{1-t}=t\sum_{a}\left(\Theta_{t|m|^{2}}\right)_{ab}

Thus we can replace
(\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}}-1)_{c_{1}c_{2}}
in (
3.57
) with
t(\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}})_{c_{1}c_{2}}
.
This replacement shows that after summing over
c_{1}
, an internal edge
c_{1}-c_{2}
edge becomes an external edge with a factor
t
. This will be crucial later on when we split the graph. We can now rewrite

(3.58)

\displaystyle\sum_{\textbf{a}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})\;=\;

\displaystyle t\cdot f^{*}(1)\cdot\sum_{a_{m},\cdots,a_{n}}\;\sum_{d_{m},\cdots,d_{n}}\;\sum_{\textbf{c}}

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{i=m}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)\cdot\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}}\right)_{c_{1}c_{2}}\prod_{k=2}^{M-1}\Big(\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}-1\Big)_{c_{2k-1}c_{2k}}\cdot\prod_{k=2}^{M}{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{d}^{(k)})

Define
\pi^{\prime}
to be
\pi
with the pair for edge
\{c_{1},c_{2}\}
removed, i.e.

\pi^{\prime}=\pi\setminus\{\{1,m\}\}

Denote

\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}=(\sigma_{1},\,\sigma_{m},\,\sigma_{m},\,\cdots\,\sigma_{n}),\quad\textbf{a}^{\prime}=(c_{1},\,a_{m},\,a_{m+1},\,\cdots\,a_{n})

Then we have (see Figure
11
for an example)

(3.59)

\displaystyle\sum_{\textbf{a}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=t\cdot f^{*}(1)

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{\textbf{a}^{\prime}}{\cal K}^{(\pi^{\prime})}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{a}^{\prime})

Notice that
\{c_{1},c_{2}\}
is a boundary edge in

\mathcal{K}^{\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)}\left(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\right)
and it needs to be of the form
\left(\Theta_{t|m|^{2}}^{(B)}\right)_{c_{1}c_{2}}
.
Since
\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}^{n-m+2}
, we can apply induction assumption (
3.50
) to
\cal K^{(\pi)^{\prime}}
. Thus

(3.60)

\displaystyle\sum_{\textbf{a}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})={O(t\cdot f^{*}(1)\cdot L\cdot(\eta_{t})^{-n+m-1})}

Multiplying
(\Theta^{(B)}_{t}|m|^{2})_{c_{1},a}=(\Theta^{(B)}_{t}m_{1}m_{m})_{c_{1},a}
to
f^{*}(c_{1})
and summing up
c_{1}
and
a
, we obtain

(3.61)

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\sum_{c_{1}}(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{1}m_{m}})_{c_{1},a}\cdot f^{*}(c_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{a,\,c_{1}}\;\sum_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m-1}}\sum_{d_{1},\cdots,d_{m-1}}\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}\Bigg(t,(\sigma_{1},\cdots\sigma_{m}),(d_{1},\cdots,d_{m-1},c_{1})\Bigg)\cdot\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{1}m_{m}})_{c_{1},a}

By definition, the right hand side can be written in terms of
{\cal K}^{(\emptyset)}\big(t,(\sigma_{1},\cdots\sigma_{m}),(a_{1},\cdots,a_{m-1},a)\big)
, namely,

(3.62)

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\sum_{c_{1}}f^{*}(c_{1})(\Theta^{(B)}_{t})_{c_{1},a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m-1},\;a}{\cal K}^{(\emptyset)}\Bigg(t,(\sigma_{1},\cdots\sigma_{m}),(a_{1},\cdots,a_{m-1},a)\Bigg).

By inductive assumption on (
3.50
), the right hand side of (
3.62
) is equal to
O(L\cdot\eta_{t}^{-m+1})
. Thus

(3.63)

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\sum_{c_{1}}f^{*}(c_{1})(\Theta^{(B)}_{t})_{c_{1},a}=O(L\cdot\eta_{t}^{-m+1})

On the other hand, since
f^{*}(c_{1})=f^{*}(1)
, we have the identity

(3.64)

\displaystyle\sum_{a}\sum_{c_{1}}f^{*}(c_{1})(\Theta^{(B)}_{t})_{c_{1},a}=L\cdot f^{*}(c_{1})\cdot(1-t)^{-1}.

Hence

f^{*}(c_{1})=f^{*}(1)=O(\eta_{t}^{-m+2})

Together with (
3.60
), we have proved (
3.50
) if
\pi\neq\emptyset
.

For
\pi=\emptyset
, we write
{\cal K}^{(\emptyset)}
as

(3.65)

\displaystyle\sum_{\textbf{a}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}{\cal K}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=\sum_{\pi}\sum_{\textbf{a}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})-\sum_{\pi\neq\emptyset}\sum_{\textbf{a}\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}}{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=O(L\cdot\eta_{t}^{-n+1})

Here we have used (
3.49
) to bound the first term on the right hand side and (
3.50
) for the second term.
We have thus proved (
3.50
) and Lemma
3.10
.

∎

3.5
Proof of Lemma
3.11

Proof of Lemma
3.11
.

We first focus the case
\pi=\emptyset
. By symmetry, without loss of generality, we can split it into three cases

1.

Pure loop, i.e.,
\sigma_{k}=+
for all
1\leq k\leq n
or
\sigma_{k}=-
for all
1\leq k\leq n
. In this case, all edges are short edges, hence we can easily obtain (
3.45
).

2.

\sigma_{1}=+
,
\sigma_{2}=-
, and there exists
j
s.t.
\sigma_{j}=\sigma_{j+1}=+
.

3.

\boldsymbol{\sigma}
is alternative as in (
3.10
), i.e.
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(alt)}
.

Recall that for
\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{2}
, and
\pi=\emptyset
, we have

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{(\emptyset)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{\textbf{d}}\left(\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})\right)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}}\right)_{a_{1},d_{1}}\cdot\sum_{d_{2},\,\cdots,\,d_{n}}\left(\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})\right)\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}

We are going to prove the following statement, which is slightly stronger than (
3.45
):

(3.66)

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{2},\,\cdots,\,d_{n}}\left(\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})\right)\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}=O(\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-n+2}\cdot\left(\min_{2\leq k\leq n}\|a_{k}-d_{1}\|+1\right)^{-1}

First, if there exists
2\leq j\leq n
s.t.
\sigma_{j}=\sigma_{j+1}=+
, then (
2.52
) shows that

\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{j}m_{j+1}}\right)_{a_{j},d_{j}}=\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm^{2}}\right)_{a_{j},d_{j}}=O(1)

It implies that

\prod_{i=2}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}=O(\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-n+2}

On the other hand, due to the short range property of
\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})
(in (
3.43
)), we have

\sum_{d_{2},\,\cdots,\,d_{n}}\left(\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})\right)\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}\leq C\prod_{i=2}^{n}\left\|\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right\|_{\max}

By combining these two bounds, we derive (
3.66
) for this case.

Next we prove (
3.66
) in the case that
\pi=\emptyset
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(alt)}
.
In this case,

\prod_{i=2}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}=\prod_{i=2}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}

With short range property of
\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})
(in (
3.43
)), and the estimate of
(\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}})_{a_{i}d_{i}}
in (
2.52
), one can easily bound the l.h.s. of (
3.66
) with

O(\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-n+1}
. To obtain the missing factor for (
3.66
), we need to apply the sum zero property which we proved in Lemma
3.10
, i.e.,

(3.67)

\sum_{d_{2},\,\cdots,\,d_{n}}\left(\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})\right)=O(\eta_{t})

For simplicity, for fixed
d_{1}
and
a
, we temporally denote
s
,
f
and
g
as follows

s_{i}:=d_{i}-d_{1},\quad\quad f(a_{i},s_{i}):=\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m|^{2}}\right)_{a_{i},(d_{1}+s_{i})},\quad g(s_{2},\cdots,s_{n}):=\Sigma^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})

Then the l.h.s. of (
3.66
) can be written as

(3.68)

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{2},\,\cdots,\,d_{n}}\left(\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})\right)\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}=\sum_{d_{1}}\sum_{s_{2}\,\cdots,\,s_{n}}\prod_{i=2}^{n}f(a_{i},s_{i})\cdot g(\textbf{{s}})

Note due the fast decay of
g(\textbf{{s}})
, we can focus on the case that
\max_{i}|s_{i}|\prec 1
.
For each
i
, we write

f(a_{i},s_{i})=f_{0}(a_{i},s_{i})+f_{1}(a_{i},s_{i})+f_{2}(a_{i},s_{i})

where

(3.69)

\displaystyle f_{0}(a_{i},s_{i})=

\displaystyle f(a_{i},0)

\displaystyle f_{1}(a_{i},s_{i})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(f(a_{i},s_{i})-f(a_{i},-s_{i})\right)

\displaystyle f_{2}(a_{i},s_{i})=

\displaystyle f(a_{i},s_{i})-f(a_{i},0)-\frac{1}{2}\left(f(a_{i},s_{i})-f(a_{i},-s_{i})\right)

By Lemma
2.14

(3.70)

\displaystyle f_{0}(a_{i},s_{i})=

\displaystyle O(\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-1},

\displaystyle f_{1}(a_{i},s_{i})=

\displaystyle O(\ell_{t}^{-1}\eta_{t}^{-1/2}),

\displaystyle f_{2}(a_{i},s_{i})=

\displaystyle O(\|a_{i}-d_{1}\|^{-1}).

Inserting

\prod_{i}f(a_{i},s_{i})=\prod_{i}\left(f_{0}(a_{i},s_{i})+f_{1}(a_{i},s_{i})+f_{2}(a_{i},s_{i})\right)

into (
3.68
), we obtain

(3.71)

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{2},\,\cdots,\,d_{n}}\left(\Sigma^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{d})\right)\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i},d_{i}}=\sum_{s_{2}\,\cdots,\,s_{n}}\;\sum_{0\leq\xi_{2},\,\xi_{3},\cdots,\xi_{n}\leq 2}\;\prod_{i=2}^{n}f_{\xi_{i}}(a_{i},s_{i})\cdot g(\textbf{{s}})

We claim that for any fixed
\xi
’s,

(3.72)

\sum_{s_{2}\,\cdots,\,s_{n}}\prod_{i=2}^{n}f_{\xi_{i}}(a_{i},s_{i})\cdot g(\textbf{{s}})=O(\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-n+2}\cdot\left(\min_{2\leq k\leq n}\|a_{k}-d_{1}\|+1\right)^{-1}.

We estimate the r.h.s. in the following cases:

•

If one of
\xi_{k}=2
, without loss of generality, let
\xi_{n}=2
, then

\prod_{i=2}^{n}f_{\xi_{i}}(a_{i},s_{i})=O\left(\ell_{t}\eta_{t}\right)^{-n+2}\cdot\|a_{n}-d_{1}\|^{-1}

It implies that in this case (
3.72
) holds.

•

If
0\leq\xi_{k}\leq 1
for all
2\leq k\leq n
, and there two of
\xi_{k}
equal to
1
. Similar to above case,

\prod_{i=2}^{n}f_{\xi_{i}}(a_{i},s_{i})=O\left(\ell_{t}\eta_{t}\right)^{-n+1}\cdot\eta_{t}=O\left(\ell_{t}\eta_{t}\right)^{-n+2}\cdot\ell_{t}^{-1}

If
\|a_{k}-d_{1}\|=O(\ell_{t})
for all
2\leq k\leq n
, this implies (
3.72
); if
\|a_{k}-d_{1}\|\gg\ell_{t}
for some
2\leq k\leq n
, then the l.h.s. of (
3.72
) is exponentially small.

•

If
0\leq\xi_{k}\leq 1
for all
2\leq k\leq n
, and only one of
\xi_{k}
equal to
1
, without loss of generality, let
\xi_{n}=1
. Similar to above case,

\prod_{i=2}^{n}f_{\xi_{i}}(a_{i},s_{i})g(\textbf{s})=\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n-1}f(a_{i},0)f_{1}(a_{n},s_{n})g(\textbf{s})

By the definition, we know

f_{1}(a_{n},s_{n})=-f_{1}(a_{n},-s_{n})

and due to the symmetric, we have

g(\textbf{s})=g(-\textbf{s})

Then by symmetry, the r.h.s. of (
3.72
) equals to zero in this case.

•

At last, if
\xi_{k}=0
for all
2\leq k\leq n
. Then

\prod_{i=2}^{n}f_{\xi_{i}}(a_{i},s_{i})g(\textbf{s})=\prod_{i=2}^{n}f(a_{i},0)g(\textbf{s})

Applying (
3.67
), we have

\sum_{\textbf{s}}g(\textbf{s})=O(\eta_{t})

It implies that in this case (
3.72
) holds.

Therefore, we have proved that for any
\xi
’s, the (
3.72
) holds, which complete the proof of (
3.66
) for the case of
\pi=\emptyset
and
\sigma_{1}=+
,
\sigma_{2}=-
.

We now prove (
3.45
) in the case
\pi\neq\emptyset
. As above, we use the decomposition method as in Figure
11
. By (
3.53
) we write

(3.73)

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})=\sum_{\textbf{d}}\sum_{\textbf{c}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{M-1}\Big(\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}-1\Big)_{c_{2k-1}c_{2k}}\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{M}{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{d}^{(k)})

Among the molecules in this molecule structure, there must be one molecule containing only one
c-
vertex, i.e., the big dot of this molecule connects with only one
(\Theta^{(B)}_{t|m^{2}|-1})_{c_{2k-1},c_{2k}}
edge. (For example, the top, left and right molecules in Figure
11
). For simplicity, we assume that it is the first molecule, and it contains
c_{1}
and connects with
a_{1},a_{2}\cdots a_{m-1}
. Note that in this case

(3.74)

\{1,m\}\in\pi,\quad\sigma_{1}\neq\sigma_{m}

As in the r.h.s of Figure
11
, we decompose
{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})
into a product of two parts,
{\cal A}
and
{\cal B}
, such that

(3.75)

\displaystyle{\cal K}^{(\pi)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a})

\displaystyle=\sum_{c_{1}}\left({\cal A}_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m-1},c_{1}}\right)\cdot\left({\cal B}_{c_{1},a_{m},\cdots,a_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m-1},c_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{d_{1},\,\cdots,\,d_{m-1}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)\cdot{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(1)},\textbf{d}^{(1)}),\quad\textbf{d}^{(1)}=(d_{1},d_{2}\cdots,d_{m-1},c_{1})

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{c_{1},a_{m},\cdots,a_{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{d_{m},\cdots,d_{n}}\;\sum_{c_{2},\cdots,c_{2M-2}}\left(\prod_{i=m}^{n}\left(\Theta^{(B)}_{tm_{i}m_{i+1}}\right)_{a_{i}d_{i}}\right)\cdot\prod_{k=1}^{M-1}\Big(\Theta_{{t|m|^{2}}}-1\Big)_{c_{2k-1}c_{2k}}\cdot\prod_{k=2}^{M}{\Sigma}^{(\emptyset)}(t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{d}^{(k)}).

Using (
3.66
), we have

{\cal A}_{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m-1},c_{1}}=O(\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-m+2}\cdot\left(\min_{1\leq k\leq m}\|a_{k}-c_{1}\|+1\right)^{-1}

On the other hand, with inductive assumption and
(\Theta_{t}-1)_{c_{1}c_{2}}=t\left(S^{(B)}\cdot\Theta_{t}\right)_{c_{1}c_{2}}
, we have

{\cal B}_{c_{1},a_{m},\cdots,a_{n}}=O(\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-n+m-1}

Combining these two bounds and inserting them back to (
3.75
), we obtain (
3.45
) for the case that
\pi\neq\emptyset
, and complete the proof of Lemma
3.11

∎

4
G
-entry estimates

In this section, we estimate
G
entries and 1-loops with the 2-loops. The proof of the following Lemma
4.1
will be via a time-independent method.
It relies on a standard decomposition widely employed for Wigner matrices (e.g.,
[
23
]
) and band matrices (e.g.,
[
19
]
).

Lemma 4.1

(Resolvent entry estimate)
.

Recall
z_{t}
and
G_{t}
(which depend on
N
) from Definitions
2.7
and
2.9
.
Suppose that
|E|<2-\kappa,0\leq t<1
.
For a fixed constant
c>0
, define the event

(4.1)

\Omega(t,c):=\big\{\|G_{t}-m\|_{\max}\leq W^{-c}\big\}.

Then the entries of
G_{t}
can be bounded in terms of
2
-
G
-loops as follows:

(4.2)

{\bf 1}_{\Omega(t,c)}\cdot\max_{i\in{\cal I}_{a}}\max_{j\in{\cal I}_{b}}|(G_{t})_{ij}|^{\,2}\prec\sum_{a^{\prime}=a-1}^{a+1}\;\sum_{b^{\prime}=b-1}^{b+1}{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}+W^{-1}\cdot{\bf 1}(|a-b|\leq 1),

(4.3)

{\bf 1}_{\Omega(t,c)}\cdot\max_{i}\left|(G_{t})_{ii}-m\right|^{\,2}\prec\max_{a,b}{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}.

In particular, if for some
c>0
,

(4.4)

\|G_{t}-m\|_{\max}\prec W^{-c},

then (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) hold without the indicator
{\bf 1}_{\Omega(t,c)}
.
Furthermore, under the same assumption, the
1
-loop estimate (interpreted as an average local law) holds:

(4.5)

\max_{a}\left|\left\langle\left(G_{t}-m\right)E_{a}\right\rangle\right|\prec\max_{a,b}{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}.

We first recall the following perturbation formulas from Lemma 4.2 of
[
22
]
:

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
H
be a Hermitian matrix and
H^{(i)}
denote the
N-1
by
N-1
submatrix of
H
after removing the
i-th
rows and columns. Define

(4.6)

\displaystyle G_{kl}^{(i)}:=\left[H^{(i)}-z\right]^{-1}(k,l).

Then we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle G_{ii}

\displaystyle\;\;=\left(H_{ii}-z-\sum_{kl}H_{ik}G^{(i)}_{kl}H_{li}\right)^{-1},

(4.8)

\displaystyle G_{ij}

\displaystyle\;\;=G_{ii}\sum_{k}H_{ik}G^{(i)}_{kj},

(4.9)

\displaystyle G^{(i)}_{jk}

\displaystyle\;\;=G_{jk}-\frac{G_{ji}G_{ik}}{G_{ii}}.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

For simplicity, we ignore the subscript
t
in our proof and write
\Omega=\Omega(t,c)
. For
i\neq j
, using (
4.8
), we have

|G_{ij}|=|G_{ii}|\left|\sum_{k}H_{ik}G^{(i)}_{kj}\right|.

Here
\mathbb{E}|H_{ij}|^{2}=t\cdot S_{ij}\leq S_{ij}
. By definition in (
4.4
), we have
{\bf 1}_{\Omega}|G_{ii}|=O(1)
.
Since
G^{(i)}
is independent of the
i
-th row of
H
, we can apply Lemma 3.3 in
[
23
]
to get

\left|\sum_{k}H_{ik}G^{(i)}_{kj}\right|\prec\left(\sum_{k}S_{ik}\left|G^{(i)}_{kj}\right|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Using (
4.9
), we further deduce that

{\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot|G^{(i)}_{kj}|\leq|G_{kj}|+O\left(W^{-c}|G_{ij}|\right).

Combining these estimates, we obtain

(4.10)

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot|G_{ij}|^{2}

\displaystyle\;\prec\;\sum_{k}S_{ik}\left|G_{kj}\right|^{2}+O\left(W^{-c}|G_{ij}|^{2}\right)\;\prec\;\sum_{k}S_{ik}\left|G_{kj}\right|^{2}.

Applying this bound on
G_{kj}
and iterating the process (for the special case
k=j
, we can bound
G_{kk}\prec 1
), we find

(4.11)

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot|G_{ij}|^{2}

\displaystyle\;\prec\;\sum_{kl}S_{ik}\left|G_{kl}\right|^{2}S_{lj}+W^{-1}{\bf 1}\Big(|[j]-[i]|\leq 1\Big).

This implies (
4.2
).

Now we prove (
4.3
). Using (
4.7
) and Lemma 3.3 in
[
23
]
, we get

{\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot\sum_{kl}H_{ik}G^{(i)}_{kl}H_{li}=\sum_{k}S_{ik}G^{(i)}_{kk}+O_{\prec}\left(\sum_{kl}S_{ik}\left|G^{(i)}_{kl}\right|^{2}S_{li}\right)^{1/2}.

As above, using (
4.9
) to remove the
(i)
superscript and applying (
4.2
), we find

{\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot\sum_{kl}H_{ik}G^{(i)}_{kl}H_{li}={\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot\sum_{k}S_{ik}G_{kk}+O_{\prec}\left({\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot{\cal E}\right),

where

{\cal E}^{2}={\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot\max_{a,b}{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}+\left(\max_{a,b}{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}\right)^{2}+W^{-1}.

On the other hand, it is easy to verify that in
\Omega(t,c)
,

cW^{-1}\leq\max_{a,b}{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}\leq CW^{-1}+W^{-c}.

Substituting back into (
4.7
), we get

{\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot G_{ii}={\bf 1}_{\Omega}\left(-z-\sum_{k}S_{ik}G_{kk}-O_{\prec}\left(\max_{a,b}{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}\right)^{1/2}\right)^{-1}.

By definition, one can easily check that
m=-(m+z)^{-1}
. Expanding the right-hand side around
(-z-m)^{-1}
, we have

{\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot\left(G_{ii}-m\right)=\sum_{j}\left[(1-m^{2}S)^{-1}\right]_{ij}\cdot{\cal E}_{j},\quad{\cal E}_{j}\prec\left(\max_{a,b}{\cal L}_{t,(+,-),(a,b)}\right)^{1/2}+W^{-c}\cdot\max_{j}|G_{jj}-m|.

Together with the fact that
\|(1-m^{2}S)^{-1}\|_{\max\to\max}=O(1)
, we conclude (
4.3
).

Next, we prove (
4.5
), which is a type of estimate commonly referred to as fluctuation averaging. A brief historical context is provided in Section 10.3.1 of
[
21
]
. In particular, very similar results are established in equation (3.7) of
[
19
]
and equation (4.11) of
[
25
]
.

Recall the definition of
H^{(i)}
introduced above (
4.6
). Denote
\mathbb{E}_{i}[X]=\mathbb{E}[X\mid H^{(i)}]
, i.e., the conditional expectation with respect to the
i
-th row and column of
H
. Previously, we showed that

\|G_{ij}-m\|_{\max}^{2}\prec\Psi^{2}:=\max_{\mathbf{a}}\mathcal{L}_{t,(+,-),\mathbf{a}},

where
\Psi
aligns with the notation in
[
25
]
. It is established in equation (4.11) of
[
25
]
that, for any
\{t_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}}
satisfying

0\leq\left|t_{k}\right|\leq W^{-1},\quad\sum_{k}\left|t_{k}\right|\leq 1,

we have

(4.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{k}t_{k}(1-\mathbb{E}_{k})(G_{kk}-m)\prec\Psi^{2}=\max_{\mathbf{a}}\mathcal{L}_{t,(+,-),\mathbf{a}}.

On the other hand, using the identity
G-m=m(H-m)G
and applying Gaussian integration by parts, we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{i}(G_{ii}-m)=\mathbb{E}_{i}[m(H-m)G]=\sum_{k}\mathbb{E}_{i}[m(G_{kk}-m)S_{ki}G_{ii}]=m^{2}\sum_{k}S_{ik}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{i}(G_{kk}-m)+O_{\prec}(\Psi^{2}).

Using (
4.9
), we deduce that

\mathbb{E}_{i}(G_{kk}-m)=\mathbb{E}_{i}(G_{kk}^{(i)}-m)+O_{\prec}(\Psi^{2})=(G_{kk}^{(i)}-m)+O_{\prec}(\Psi^{2})=G_{kk}-m+O_{\prec}(\Psi^{2}).

Substituting this into the earlier equation, we find

\mathbb{E}_{i}(G_{ii}-m)=m^{2}\sum_{k}S_{ik}\cdot(G_{kk}-m)+O_{\prec}(\Psi^{2})=m^{2}\sum_{k}S_{ik}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{k}(G_{kk}-m)+O_{\prec}(\Psi^{2}),

where the last estimate follows from (
4.12
). Solving this equation, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{i}(G_{ii}-m)=O_{\prec}(\Psi^{2}).

Combining this with (
4.12
), we derive (
4.5
) with
t_{k}=W^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{1}(k\in\mathcal{I}_{a})
.

∎

5
Analysis of loop hierarchy

In this section, we prove Theorem
2.21
. Except for Step 1, the proof primarily relies on analyzing the G-loop hierarchy.

5.1
Proof of Theorem
2.21
, step 1

Proof.

Our goal is to establish (
2.73
) and (
2.74
). Using the assumption (
2.72
) and the definitions
\ell_{t}=\ell(z_{t})
and
\eta_{t}=\im z_{t}
, we have

1-u\gg N^{-1},\quad\eta_{u}\gg N^{-1},\quad u\geq t.

Given that
\partial_{z}(H-z)^{-1}=(H-z)^{-2}
and the entries of
H_{t}
follow a Gaussian distribution, it follows that for any
C>0
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
such that

(5.1)

\max_{u\geq N^{-1}}\max_{|u-u^{\prime}|\leq N^{-C^{\prime}}}\|G_{u}-G_{u^{\prime}}\|_{\max}\leq N^{-C},

holds up to events that are exponentially small (negligible). Hence, through a standard
N^{-C}
net argument, we can reduce the proof of (
2.73
) and (
2.74
) for all
u
to the case
u=t
. This standard procedure, which we will refer to as a continuity argument, will be used repeatedly in this paper. We now focus on the proof for the case
u=t
.

Case 1:
s<t\leq 1/2
.

Under this assumption on
t
,

\|G_{t}\|_{op}=1/\eta_{t}=O(1).

Combined with the fact that
\|E_{a}\|_{op}\leq W^{-1}
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{L}
, we have

(5.2)

{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}G_{t}(\sigma_{i})E_{a_{i}}\right\rangle=O(W^{-n+1}),\quad t\leq 1/2,

which implies (
2.73
) for
t\leq 1/2
. Applying (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) from Lemma
4.1
to
\|G_{t}-m\|_{\max}
, and using the bound
\cal L
in (
5.2
), we find that for
t\leq 1/2
,

{\bf 1}\left(\|G_{t}-m\|_{\max}\leq W^{-1/10}\right)\cdot\|G_{t}-m\|_{\max}\prec W^{-1/2},\quad t\leq 1/2,

where we have used the fact that
\ell_{t}
and
\eta_{t}
are of order one. On the other hand, using the assumption (
2.70
) for
s
, we have
\|G_{s}-m\|\prec W^{-1/2}
. Then, applying a standard continuity argument (with an
N^{-C}
net between
s
and
t
) and (
5.1
), we obtain that for any
u\in[s,t]
,

(5.3)

\|G_{u}-m\|_{\max}\prec W^{-1/2},\quad u\leq 1/2.

Hence, we have proved (
2.73
) and (
2.74
) for the case
t\leq 1/2
.

Case 2:
t>s\geq 1/2
.

Combining the assumption (
2.68
) and (
2.59
) from Lemma
2.17
, i.e.,

{\cal K}_{s,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\prec(W\ell_{s}\eta_{s})^{-n+1},

we obtain that for any fixed
n
and
s
,

(5.4)

{\cal L}_{s,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=O((W\ell_{s}\eta_{s})^{-n+1}).

The following lemma will be proved in Section
6
.

Lemma 5.1

(Continuity estimate on loops)
.

Suppose that
c<t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq 1
for some constant
c>0
. Assume that for any fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the following bounds hold at time
t_{1}
:

(5.5)

\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}{\cal L}_{t_{1},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\prec\left(W\ell_{1}\eta_{1}\right)^{-n+1},\quad\eta_{i}=\eta_{t_{i}},\quad\ell_{i}=\ell_{t_{i}}.

Define
\Omega
as

(5.6)

\Omega:=\left\{\|G_{t_{2}}\|_{\max}\leq 2\right\}.

Then, for any fixed
n\geq 1
, we have

(5.7)

{\bf 1}_{\Omega}\cdot\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}{\cal L}_{t_{2},\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\prec\left(W\ell_{1}\eta_{2}\right)^{-n+1}=\left(\frac{\ell_{2}}{\ell_{1}}\right)^{n-1}\cdot\left(W\ell_{2}\eta_{2}\right)^{-n+1},\quad\eta_{i}=\eta_{t_{i}},\quad\ell_{i}=\ell_{t_{i}}.

Using (
5.2
) and (
5.4
), we know that the estimate (
5.5
) for
{\cal L}_{s}
holds. Applying Lemma
5.1
, we obtain

(5.8)

{\bf 1}\left(\|G_{u}\|_{\max}\leq 2\right)\cdot\max_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}{\cal L}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\prec\left(\frac{\ell_{u}}{\ell_{s}}\right)^{n-1}\cdot\left(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u}\right)^{-n+1},\quad s\leq u\leq t.

Combining the special case
n=2
of (
5.8
) with Lemma
4.1
, we have

{\bf 1}\left(\|G_{u}-m\|_{\max}\leq(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-1/6}\right)\cdot\|G_{u}-m\|_{\max}\prec\left(\frac{\ell_{u}}{\ell_{s}}\right)^{1/2}\cdot\left(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u}\right)^{-1/2},\quad s\leq u\leq t.

Using the assumption (
2.72
), we find that for any fixed
D>0
,

(5.9)

\mathbb{P}\left((W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-1/4}\leq\|G_{u}-m\|_{\max}\leq(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-1/6}\right)\leq N^{-D},\quad s\leq u\leq t.

This shows that the interval
\big((W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-1/4},(W\ell_{u}\eta_{u})^{-1/6}\big)
is a forbidden region for
\|G_{u}-m\|_{\max}
for any
u
between
s
and
t
. Since this event holds with very high probability, the standard continuity argument implies that it holds for all time between
s
and
t
simultaneously.

On the other hand, by assumption and (
5.3
), we have the initial bound at time
s
:

\|G_{s}-m\|_{\max}\prec(W\ell_{s}\eta_{s})^{-1/2}.

Using a standard continuity argument between
s
and
t
, we conclude

\|G_{t}-m\|_{\max}\prec(W\ell_{t}\eta_{t})^{-1/4}.

Combining this with (
5.8
), we have proved (
2.73
) in this case.

Case 3:
t>1/2>s
.

From Case 1, we know that (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) hold for
t=1/2
. Note that the proof of Case 2 uses only these two conditions. Therefore, the current case follows as a consequence of Case 2 with
s=1/2
.

∎

5.2
Dynamics of
\cal L-\cal K

Recall the loop hierarchy from Lemma
2.11
. Using the notations

\mathcal{L}_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(a),\,L}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}:=\left(\mathcal{G}^{(a),L}_{k,l}\circ\mathcal{L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right),\quad\mathcal{L}_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(b),\,R}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}:=\left(\mathcal{G}^{(b),R}_{k,l}\circ\mathcal{L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\right),

the hierarchy takes the compact form:

(5.10)

\displaystyle d\mathcal{L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\mathcal{E}^{(\widetilde{G})}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+W\cdot\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sum_{a,b}\mathcal{L}_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(a),\,L}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot\mathcal{L}_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(b),\,R}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}\,dt.

The primitive hierarchy governing the evolution of
\mathcal{K}
is given by:

(5.11)

\displaystyle d\mathcal{K}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=W\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(a),\,L}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(b),\,R}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}\,dt.

Combining the two equations, we obtain:

\displaystyle d(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=

\displaystyle\;W\cdot\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sum_{a,b}\Big((\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(a),\,L}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(b),\,R}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}+\left(\mathcal{K}\iff\mathcal{L-K}\right)\Big)\,dt

(5.12)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{E}^{((\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\times(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K}))}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\mathcal{E}^{(\widetilde{G})}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}},

where

(5.13)

\mathcal{E}^{((\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\times(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K}))}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}:=W\cdot\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sum_{a,b}(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(a),\,L}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(b),\,R}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}\,dt.

Here
\mathcal{K}\iff\mathcal{L-K}
represents the terms obtained by swapping
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{L-K}
. The terms on the first line of the right-hand side correspond to
(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})
”loops” connected with a
\mathcal{K}
”loop” via
S^{(B)}_{ab}
. These terms can be rearranged by the rank of
\mathcal{K}
, i.e., the length of the corresponding
G
-loop. It
allows us to rewrite the first line of (
5.2
) as:

\displaystyle\eqref{eq_L-K-1}=\sum_{l_{\cal K}=2}^{n}

\displaystyle\;W\cdot\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq n}\sum_{a,b}\Big((\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(a),\,L}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}\cdot S^{(B)}_{ab}\cdot\mathcal{K}_{t,\;\mathcal{G}^{(b),\,R}_{k,\,l}\left(\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a}\right)}\cdot\textbf{1}(\text{length of $\mathcal{K}$ loop equals }l_{\mathcal{K}})

(5.14)

\displaystyle+\left(\mathcal{K}\iff\mathcal{L-K}\right)\Big):=\sum_{l_{\cal K}=2}^{n}\Big[\mathcal{K}\sim(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\Big]^{l_{\mathcal{K}}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}.

Separating the special case
l_{\mathcal{K}}=2
, we have
the
\cal L-K
hierarchy

(5.15)

\displaystyle d(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=

\displaystyle\Big[\mathcal{K}\sim(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\Big]^{l_{\mathcal{K}}=2}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\sum_{l_{\mathcal{K}}>2}\Big[\mathcal{K}\sim(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\Big]^{l_{\mathcal{K}}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\mathcal{E}^{((\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\times(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K}))}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}+\mathcal{E}^{(\widetilde{G})}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}.

Clearly, we can view
\Big[\mathcal{K}\sim(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\Big]^{l_{\mathcal{K}}=2}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
as a linear transform of the tensor
(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
.

Definition 5.2

(Definition of
{\varTheta}_{\,t,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
and
{\cal U}_{\,s,\,t,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
)
.

Define the linear operator
{\varTheta}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
on a tensor
{\cal A}:\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{L}\to\mathbb{C}
by

\displaystyle\left({{\varTheta}}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{A}\right)_{\textbf{a}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{b_{i}}\left(\frac{m_{i}m_{i+1}}{1-tm_{i}m_{i+1}S^{(B)}}\right)_{a_{i}b_{i}}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{a}^{(i)}},\quad m_{i}=m(\sigma_{i})

(5.16)

\displaystyle\quad\textbf{a}^{(i)}

\displaystyle=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},b_{i},a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n}).

We also define the evolution kernel

(5.17)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{U}_{s,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{A}\right)_{\textbf{a}}=\sum_{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1-s\cdot m_{i}m_{i+1}S^{(B)}}{1-t\cdot m_{i}m_{i+1}S^{(B)}}\right)_{a_{i}b_{i}}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{b}},\quad\textbf{b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}),\quad m_{i}=m(\sigma_{i})

For any
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

(5.18)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1-s\xi S^{(B)}}{1-t\xi S^{(B)}}\right)=I-(s-t)\xi\cdot\Theta^{(B)}_{t\xi}\cdot S^{(B)}.

Using these notations and the rank-2
\mathcal{K}
tensor from (
2.57
), we derive the following identity for the first term on the right-hand side of (
5.15
).

(5.19)

\displaystyle\left({\varTheta}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\right)_{\textbf{a}}=\Big[\mathcal{K}\sim(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\Big]^{l_{\mathcal{K}}=2}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}.

The following lemma is just a form of Duhamel formula.

Lemma 5.3

(Integrated loop hierarchy)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{t}
be a tensor satisfying the stochastic equation:

d\mathcal{A}_{t}={\varTheta}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{A}_{t}\;dt+\mathcal{D}_{t}\;dt+\mathcal{C}_{t}\cdot d{\cal B}_{t}.

Then for
s<t
the
\mathcal{A}_{t}
satisfies

\mathcal{A}_{t}=\mathcal{U}_{s,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{A}_{s}+\int_{s}^{t}\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{D}_{u}\;du+\int_{s}^{t}\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\left(\mathcal{C}_{u}\cdot d{\cal B}_{u}\right).

Inserting this solution into (
5.15
), we have the following integrated loop hierarchy

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\;=\;\;

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{U}_{s,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{s,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{l_{\mathcal{K}}>2}\int_{s}^{t}\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\Big[\mathcal{K}\sim(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\Big]^{l_{\mathcal{K}}}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}du

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{t}\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{((\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\times(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K}))}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}du

(5.20)

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{t}\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(\widetilde{G})}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}du+\int_{s}^{t}\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}

Furthermore, let
T
be a stopping time with respect to the matrix Brownian motion
H_{t}
and denote
\tau:=T\wedge t
.
Then we have the stopped integrated loop hierarchy

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{\tau,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\;=\;\;

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{U}_{s,\tau,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})_{s,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{l_{\mathcal{K}}>2}\int_{s}^{\tau}\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,\tau,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\Big[\mathcal{K}\sim(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\Big]^{l_{\mathcal{K}}}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}du

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{\tau}\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,\tau,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{((\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})\times(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K}))}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}du

(5.21)

\displaystyle+\int_{s}^{\tau}\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,\tau,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(\widetilde{G})}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}du+{\cal U}_{t,\tau}\circ\int_{s}^{\tau}\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}.

The equation (
5.3
) will serve as our fundamental equation for estimating
\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K}
. To analyze this equation, we first introduce the following notation, which is necessary to compute the quadratic variation of the martingale term.

Definition 5.4

(Definition of
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}
)
.

Denote

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}\right)_{t,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a},\,\textbf{a}^{\prime}}\;:=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}\right)^{(k)}_{t,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a},\,\textbf{a}^{\prime}}

(5.22)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}\right)^{(k)}_{t,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a},\,\textbf{a}^{\prime}}:\;=\;

\displaystyle W\sum_{b,b^{\prime}}S^{(B)}_{b,b^{\prime}}{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{a}^{(k)}},\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)}\in\{+,-\}^{2n+2},

where the loop
{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)},\textbf{a}^{(k)}}
is obtained by cutting the
k
-th edge of
{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}
and then attach itself (with indices
a
) with its complex conjugate loop (with indices
\textbf{a}^{\prime}
) into a bigger loop, with the new indices
b
and
b^{\prime}
. Hence, there are
n
indices between
b
and
b^{\prime}
so that

\displaystyle\textbf{a}^{(k)}=(a_{k},a_{k+1},\cdots a_{n},a_{1},\cdots a_{k-1},b^{\prime},a^{\prime}_{k-1}\cdots a_{1}^{\prime},a_{n}^{\prime}\cdots a^{\prime}_{k},b)

(5.23)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(k)}=(\,\sigma_{k},\,\sigma_{k+1},\cdots\sigma_{n},\,\sigma_{1},\cdots\sigma_{k},\,\overline{\sigma}_{k},\cdots\overline{\sigma}_{1},\,\overline{\sigma}_{n}\cdots\overline{\sigma}_{k})

The symbol
\otimes
in the notation
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}
was used to emphasize the symmetric structure (as illustrated in Figure (
13
)); it does not denote a tensor product.

Example: For a
3\text{-}G
loop
\mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma},\mathbf{a}}
, Figure
13
represents one loop that appears in
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}
. Although
S^{(B)}
is not part of the loop, it does appear within
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}
.

a_{1}
a_{2}

b
b^{\prime}

a^{\prime}_{3}
G_{2}
G_{3}

k=1

\textbf{a}^{(1)}=(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},b^{\prime},a_{3}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime},a_{1}^{\prime},b);

\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(1)}=(\;\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\sigma_{1},\overline{\sigma_{1}},\overline{\sigma_{3}},\overline{\sigma_{2}},\overline{\sigma_{1}},).

:
Example of the
8-G
loop in
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}
: for
\sigma\in\{+,-\}^{3}

Lemma 5.5

(The martingale term)
.

For any stopping time
T
with respect to
H_{t}
and
\tau:=t\wedge T
, we have

(5.24)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{s}^{\tau}\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}\right]^{2p}\leq C_{n,p}\;\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{s}^{\tau}\left(\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\right)\;\circ\;\left(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}\right)_{u,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{{\textbf{a}},{\textbf{a}}}du\right)^{p}

where
\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is the conjugate sign vector of
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
. More precisely, as in (
5.17
),

\left[\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\right)\circ\cal A\right]_{\textbf{a},\textbf{a}^{\prime}}=\sum_{\textbf{b},\;\textbf{b}^{\prime}}\;\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1-u\cdot m_{i}m_{i+1}S^{(B)}}{1-t\cdot m_{i}m_{i+1}S^{(B)}}\right)_{a_{i}b_{i}}\;\cdot\;\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1-u\cdot\overline{m_{i}m_{i+1}}S^{(B)}}{1-t\cdot\overline{m_{i}m_{i+1}}S^{(B)}}\right)_{a^{\prime}_{i}b^{\prime}_{i}}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{\textbf{b},\textbf{b}^{\prime}}.

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

We will prove the case
\tau=t
; the general case is identical.
By Definition
2.46
,
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}=\sum_{\alpha=(i,j)}\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}(\alpha)\cdot d{\cal B}_{\alpha}(t)

and

\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}=\sum_{\alpha=(i,j)}\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\alpha)\right)_{\textbf{a}}\cdot d{\cal B}_{\alpha}(t)

Here with
\quad\alpha=(i,j)
we have

\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}(\alpha)=(S_{ij})^{1/2}\cdot\partial_{(H_{t})_{xy}}{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}.

Using the chain rule and the structure of
\cal L
, we can write

\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}(\alpha)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\;\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}(\alpha,k),\quad\quad\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}(\alpha,k):=(S_{ij})^{1/2}\cdot{\cal L}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}\Big|\left(G_{k}\to\partial_{(H_{t})_{xy}}G_{k}\right)

where
(\alpha,k)
denotes the part that the derivative acts on the
k
-th
G
edge in the
\cal L
. By definition (
5.4
), we have

\sum_{\alpha}\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}}(\alpha,k)\cdot\overline{\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{t,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\textbf{a}^{\prime}}(\alpha,k)}=\left(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}\right)^{(k)}_{t,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma},\,\textbf{a},\,\textbf{a}^{\prime}}

Since
\cal U
is a deterministic linear operator, the quadratic variation of the martingale term in (
5.3
)
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\left[\int\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{\textbf{a}}\right]_{t}\;=\;

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\sum_{\alpha}\left|\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\alpha)\right|^{2}du=\int_{s}^{t}\sum_{\alpha}\left|\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\alpha,k)\right|^{2}du

\displaystyle\;\;\leq C_{n}\;

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left|\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{(M)}_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(\alpha,k)\right|^{2}du

(5.25)

\displaystyle\;=\;C_{n}\;

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}\left(\left(\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{u,t,\overline{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\right)\;\circ\;\left(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{E}\right)_{u,\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\right)_{{\textbf{a}},{\textbf{a}}}du.

Here we have used the Schwarz inequality in expanding the square.
Our desired result, (
5.24
), follows from the BDG inequality.

∎

5.3
Proof of Theorem
2.21
, step 2

In this section, we focus on the
(+,-)

2
-
G
-loop, i.e.
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(+,-)
. The subscript
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
will be dropped in this subsection. We will prove (
2.76
) first, and (
2.75
) will be proved at the end of this section.
Define the tail functions
{\cal T}^{(\cal L-K)}_{u}(\ell)
and
{\cal T}_{u,D}(\ell)

(5.26)

{\cal T}^{(\cal L-K)}_{u}(\ell):=\max_{a,\,b\;:\;|a-b|\geq\ell}\left|({\cal L-K})_{u,\boldsymbol{\sigma},(a,b)}\right|,\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(+,-),

(5.27)
